11:T73d1,Calculus teaches how to use limits, derivatives and integrals. Real analysis asks why they work at all: what a real number is, what "gets arbitrarily close" means, and which hidden assumption makes the Intermediate Value Theorem or the convergence of $\sum 1/n^2$ true. The answer, again and again, is one property of $\mathbb{R}$ that the rationals $\mathbb{Q}$ lack: completeness, the fact that $\mathbb{R}$ has no gaps. This course rebuilds single-variable calculus on that one axiom, with proofs. It follows the standard undergraduate route of Abbott's Understanding Analysis, with Rudin's Principles of Mathematical Analysis, Bartle and Sherbert's Introduction to Real Analysis, Tao's Analysis I and Spivak's Calculus as parallel reading. This first post sets up the language of proof, the axioms of an ordered field, the supremum and the Axiom of Completeness, and its first consequences: the Archimedean property, density of $\mathbb{Q}$, the existence of $\sqrt{2}$, nested intervals, and the fact that $\mathbb{R}$ is uncountable.

> Series map: 00 The Real Numbers & Completeness (this post) → 01 Sequences & Series → 02 Topology of ℝ & Continuity → 03 Differentiation & Taylor's Theorem → 04 The Riemann Integral → 05 Uniform Convergence & Power Series.

Learning Outcome

- Read and write statements with the quantifiers "for all" ($\forall$) and "there exists" ($\exists$), and negate nested statements such as "for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is a $\delta > 0$ …" correctly.- Use the three core proof patterns — direct proof, proof by contradiction, and induction — and prove that $\sqrt{2}$ is irrational.- List the ordered field axioms and prove the triangle inequality from them.- Define upper bound, supremum and infimum, and use the $\varepsilon$-characterization of the supremum.- State the Axiom of Completeness and explain why $\mathbb{Q}$ fails it.- Prove the Archimedean property, the density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$, and the existence of $\sqrt{2}$ in $\mathbb{R}$.- State and prove the Nested Interval Property.- Show that $\mathbb{Q}$ is countable and that $\mathbb{R}$ is uncountable (Cantor's diagonal argument), and describe the Cantor set.

Chapter 1 — Logic and proof

Analysis is written in a small, precise language. Most mistakes in a first analysis course are not mistakes about numbers; they are mistakes about quantifiers.

Terminology

- Statement — a sentence that is either true or false, e.g. "$7$ is prime".- Implication "$P \Rightarrow Q$" — "if $P$ then $Q$". It is false only when $P$ is true and $Q$ is false.- Converse of $P \Rightarrow Q$ — the statement $Q \Rightarrow P$. It is a different statement and may be false.- Contrapositive of $P \Rightarrow Q$ — the statement $\neg Q \Rightarrow \neg P$. It is logically equivalent to the original.- Universal quantifier $\forall$ — "for all". Existential quantifier $\exists$ — "there exists (at least one)".- Counterexample — one specific object that shows a "for all" statement is false.

Negating quantified statements

The two rules are:

$$\neg\big(\forall x,\ P(x)\big) \iff \exists x,\ \neg P(x), \qquad \neg\big(\exists x,\ P(x)\big) \iff \forall x,\ \neg P(x).$$

To negate a nested statement, move the $\neg$ from left to right, flipping every quantifier it passes, and negate the final condition. The definition of "the sequence $(an)$ converges to $a$" (see Real Analysis 01) is

$$\forall \varepsilon > 0\ \ \exists N \in \mathbb{N}\ \ \forall n \ge N:\ \ \lvert an - a \rvert < \varepsilon.$$

Its negation, "$(an)$ does not converge to $a$", is

$$\exists \varepsilon > 0\ \ \forall N \in \mathbb{N}\ \ \exists n \ge N:\ \ \lvert an - a \rvert \ge \varepsilon.$$

In words: there is one fixed tolerance $\varepsilon$ such that, however far out you go, some later term is still at least $\varepsilon$ away from $a$.

The same mechanical rule applies to continuity (see Real Analysis 02). "$f$ is continuous at $c$" is $\forall \varepsilon > 0\ \exists \delta > 0\ \forall x:\ \lvert x - c\rvert < \delta \Rightarrow \lvert f(x) - f(c)\rvert < \varepsilon$. Its negation is

$$\exists \varepsilon > 0\ \ \forall \delta > 0\ \ \exists x:\ \ \lvert x - c \rvert < \delta \ \text{ and } \ \lvert f(x) - f(c) \rvert \ge \varepsilon.$$

Note that the negation of an implication $A \Rightarrow B$ is "$A$ and not $B$", not another implication.

> Watch out: the order of quantifiers matters. "$\forall x\ \exists y:\ y > x$" (every real number has a larger one) is true in $\mathbb{R}$. "$\exists y\ \forall x:\ y > x$" (one number is larger than all of them) is false. Swapping $\forall$ and $\exists$ changes the meaning completely. In analysis the classic example is uniform continuity (Real Analysis 02), which differs from continuity only by the position of one quantifier.

Three proof patterns

Direct proof. Assume the hypothesis, reach the conclusion by valid steps. Example: if $m$ and $n$ are even, $m + n$ is even. Write $m = 2a$, $n = 2b$; then $m + n = 2(a + b)$.

Contrapositive and contradiction. To prove $P \Rightarrow Q$ you may prove $\neg Q \Rightarrow \neg P$ instead. In a proof by contradiction you assume the statement is false and derive something impossible. Example: if $n^2$ is even, then $n$ is even. Contrapositive: if $n$ is odd, $n = 2k + 1$, then $n^2 = 4k^2 + 4k + 1 = 2(2k^2 + 2k) + 1$ is odd.

Induction. To prove $P(n)$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N} = \{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$, prove the base case $P(1)$ and the inductive step $P(n) \Rightarrow P(n+1)$. This works because of the well-ordering principle: every nonempty subset of $\mathbb{N}$ has a least element (if $P$ failed somewhere, look at the least failure).

Example (Bernoulli's inequality). For every $x \ge -1$ and every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, $(1 + x)^n \ge 1 + nx$.

Proof. For $n = 1$ both sides equal $1 + x$. Suppose $(1+x)^n \ge 1 + nx$. Since $1 + x \ge 0$, multiplying both sides by $1 + x$ keeps the inequality:

$$(1+x)^{n+1} \ge (1 + nx)(1 + x) = 1 + (n+1)x + nx^2 \ge 1 + (n+1)x,$$

because $nx^2 \ge 0$. $\blacksquare$

The hypothesis $x \ge -1$ is used exactly once, to multiply an inequality by a non-negative number. Bernoulli's inequality is the standard tool for showing $r^n \to 0$ when $\lvert r \rvert < 1$ (Real Analysis 01).

The square root of 2 is irrational

Theorem. There is no rational number $r$ with $r^2 = 2$.

Proof. Suppose, for contradiction, that $r = p/q$ with integers $p, q$, $q \ne 0$, and $r^2 = 2$. Cancel common factors, so that $p$ and $q$ are not both even. Then $p^2 = 2q^2$, so $p^2$ is even, and by the contrapositive example above $p$ is even: $p = 2k$. Substituting, $4k^2 = 2q^2$, so $q^2 = 2k^2$ is even, and $q$ is even. Now $p$ and $q$ are both even, contradicting the choice of $p/q$ in lowest terms. $\blacksquare$

This is the first sign that $\mathbb{Q}$ has gaps: the rationals whose square is less than $2$ creep up towards a point that is not there.

Chapter 2 — Ordered fields and absolute value

Terminology

- Field — a set $F$ with two operations $+$ and $\cdot$ satisfying the field axioms below.- Ordered field — a field with a relation $<$ satisfying the order axioms below.- Absolute value — $\lvert x \rvert = x$ if $x \ge 0$ and $\lvert x \rvert = -x$ if $x < 0$.- Distance between $x$ and $y$ — the number $\lvert x - y \rvert$.

The axioms

A field satisfies, for all $x, y, z$:

| Axiom | Addition | Multiplication ||-------|----------|----------------|| Closure | $x + y \in F$ | $xy \in F$ || Associative | $(x+y)+z = x+(y+z)$ | $(xy)z = x(yz)$ || Commutative | $x + y = y + x$ | $xy = yx$ || Identity | $x + 0 = x$ | $1 \cdot x = x$, with $1 \ne 0$ || Inverse | some $-x$ has $x + (-x) = 0$ | if $x \ne 0$, some $x^{-1}$ has $x x^{-1} = 1$ || Distributive | $x(y+z) = xy + xz$ | (links the two operations) |

An ordered field also has a relation $<$ such that:

1. Trichotomy: for all $x, y$ exactly one of $x < y$, $x = y$, $y < x$ holds.2. Transitivity: $x < y$ and $y < z$ imply $x < z$.3. Addition: $x < y$ implies $x + z < y + z$.4. Multiplication: $x < y$ and $0 < z$ imply $xz < yz$.

Everything in high-school algebra follows. For example, $x^2 \ge 0$ for all $x$, and $0 < 1$ (since $1 = 1^2$ and $1 \ne 0$). If $x < y$ and $z < 0$ then $yz < xz$: multiplying by a negative number flips an inequality.

Both $\mathbb{Q}$ and $\mathbb{R}$ are ordered fields. So these axioms cannot be what distinguishes them; Chapter 3 adds the one axiom that does.

The triangle inequality

Three facts about absolute value, all checked by cases on the signs: $\lvert xy \rvert = \lvert x \rvert \lvert y \rvert$; $-\lvert x \rvert \le x \le \lvert x \rvert$; and for $a \ge 0$, $\lvert x \rvert \le a \iff -a \le x \le a$.

Theorem (triangle inequality). For all real $x, y$: $\lvert x + y \rvert \le \lvert x \rvert + \lvert y \rvert$.

Proof. Add $-\lvert x \rvert \le x \le \lvert x \rvert$ and $-\lvert y \rvert \le y \le \lvert y \rvert$:

$$-(\lvert x \rvert + \lvert y \rvert) \le x + y \le \lvert x \rvert + \lvert y \rvert.$$

By the third fact with $a = \lvert x \rvert + \lvert y \rvert$, this says $\lvert x + y \rvert \le \lvert x \rvert + \lvert y \rvert$. $\blacksquare$

Two forms are used constantly:

- Distance form: $\lvert a - b \rvert \le \lvert a - c \rvert + \lvert c - b \rvert$ (put $x = a - c$, $y = c - b$). Going from $a$ to $b$ via $c$ is never shorter.- Reverse triangle inequality: $\big\lvert \lvert x \rvert - \lvert y \rvert \big\rvert \le \lvert x - y \rvert$. (From $\lvert x \rvert = \lvert (x - y) + y \rvert \le \lvert x - y \rvert + \lvert y \rvert$, and the same with $x$ and $y$ swapped.)

> Trick: almost every $\varepsilon$ proof in this course is "add and subtract a middle term, then use the triangle inequality". When you see $\lvert an bn - ab \rvert$, write it as $\lvert an bn - an b + an b - ab \rvert$ and split.

Proving that two numbers are equal

Lemma. Two real numbers $a$ and $b$ are equal if and only if for every $\varepsilon > 0$, $\lvert a - b \rvert < \varepsilon$.

Proof. If $a = b$ then $\lvert a - b \rvert = 0 < \varepsilon$. Conversely, suppose $a \ne b$. Then $\varepsilon0 = \lvert a - b \rvert > 0$, and $\lvert a - b \rvert < \varepsilon0$ is false. $\blacksquare$

This is how analysis proves equalities: not by computing both sides, but by showing their distance is smaller than any positive tolerance.

Chapter 3 — Supremum, infimum and completeness

Terminology

- Upper bound — $b$ is an upper bound of $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ if $a \le b$ for every $a \in A$. $A$ is bounded above if it has one.- Lower bound, bounded below — the same with $\ge$. Bounded means bounded above and below.- Supremum (least upper bound) — $s = \sup A$ if (i) $s$ is an upper bound of $A$, and (ii) $s \le b$ for every upper bound $b$ of $A$.- Infimum (greatest lower bound) — $\inf A$, defined the same way with the inequalities reversed.- Maximum — an element of $A$ that is an upper bound of $A$. A set may have a supremum but no maximum.

A supremum is unique when it exists: if $s1$ and $s2$ are both least upper bounds, then $s1 \le s2$ and $s2 \le s1$.

Examples

| Set $A$ | $\sup A$ | Attained (a max)? | $\inf A$ ||---------|----------|-------------------|----------|| $[0, 1]$ | $1$ | yes | $0$ || $(0, 1)$ | $1$ | no | $0$ || $\{1 - 1/n : n \in \mathbb{N}\}$ | $1$ | no | $0$ (a min) || $\{1/n : n \in \mathbb{N}\}$ | $1$ | yes | $0$ (not a min) || $\mathbb{N}$ | none (not bounded above) | — | $1$ |

For $A = \{1 - 1/n\}$: $1$ is an upper bound since $1/n > 0$. If $b < 1$, the Archimedean property (Chapter 4) gives $n$ with $1/n < 1 - b$, so $1 - 1/n > b$ and $b$ is not an upper bound. For example, $b = 0.85$ is beaten by $1 - \tfrac17 = \tfrac67 \approx 0.857$ (and $n = 7$ is the first index that works, since $1 - \tfrac16 \approx 0.833$). Hence $\sup A = 1$, and $1 \notin A$.

The epsilon characterization

Lemma. Let $s$ be an upper bound of $A$. Then $s = \sup A$ if and only if for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is some $a \in A$ with $s - \varepsilon < a$.

Proof. ($\Rightarrow$) Let $\varepsilon > 0$. Since $s - \varepsilon < s$ and $s$ is the least upper bound, $s - \varepsilon$ is not an upper bound. So some $a \in A$ has $a > s - \varepsilon$.

($\Leftarrow$) Let $b$ be any upper bound with $b < s$. Put $\varepsilon = s - b > 0$. By hypothesis some $a \in A$ has $a > s - \varepsilon = b$, contradicting that $b$ is an upper bound. So every upper bound $b$ satisfies $b \ge s$, and $s = \sup A$. $\blacksquare$

In pictures: nothing in $A$ lies to the right of $s$, but every window $(s - \varepsilon, s]$, however narrow, contains a point of $A$.

The Axiom of Completeness

> Axiom of Completeness (least upper bound property). Every nonempty set of real numbers that is bounded above has a supremum in $\mathbb{R}$.

$\mathbb{R}$ is defined to be an ordered field that satisfies this axiom. (Such a field exists and is unique up to an order-preserving isomorphism; the standard constructions use Dedekind cuts, as in Rudin Chapter 1, or Cauchy sequences of rationals, as in Tao Chapter 5. This course takes the axiom as the starting point, as Abbott does.)

The axiom for infima follows. If $A$ is nonempty and bounded below, then $-A = \{-a : a \in A\}$ is nonempty and bounded above, and $\inf A = -\sup(-A)$.

Why $\mathbb{Q}$ fails the axiom. Let $S = \{r \in \mathbb{Q} : r^2 < 2\}$. It is nonempty ($1 \in S$) and bounded above by $2$. Suppose it had a least upper bound $s$ in $\mathbb{Q}$. The argument in Chapter 4 (existence of $\sqrt{2}$) shows that $s^2 < 2$ and $s^2 > 2$ are both impossible, so $s^2 = 2$; but no rational squares to $2$. So $S$ has no supremum in $\mathbb{Q}$: the rationals have a hole exactly where $\sqrt{2}$ should be. Concretely, $1, 1.4, 1.41, 1.414, \ldots$ are in $S$ (their squares are below $2$; for example $1.414^2 = 1.999396$), while $1.5, 1.42, 1.415, \ldots$ are rational upper bounds ($1.415^2 = 2.002225$). Every rational upper bound can be improved, and the two lists close in on a point that $\mathbb{Q}$ does not contain.

Working with suprema

Two small results, each proved with the lemma above, are used often.

- Shift: for $c \in \mathbb{R}$, $\sup(A + c) = \sup A + c$, where $A + c = \{a + c : a \in A\}$. (If $s = \sup A$, then $s + c$ is an upper bound of $A + c$, and for each $\varepsilon$ some $a > s - \varepsilon$ gives $a + c > (s + c) - \varepsilon$.)- Order: if $A \subseteq B$, $A$ is nonempty and $B$ is bounded above, then $\sup A \le \sup B$. (Every upper bound of $B$ is an upper bound of $A$.)

> Common pitfall: assuming $\sup A \in A$. The supremum of $(0,1)$ is $1$, which is not in the set. Proofs that pick "the largest element of $A$" are wrong unless $A$ is finite or known to have a maximum.

Chapter 4 — First consequences of completeness

The Archimedean property

Theorem (Archimedean property).(i) For every $x \in \mathbb{R}$ there is $n \in \mathbb{N}$ with $n > x$.(ii) For every $y > 0$ there is $n \in \mathbb{N}$ with $1/n < y$.

Proof. (i) Suppose not: then $x$ is an upper bound of $\mathbb{N}$, so by completeness $\alpha = \sup \mathbb{N}$ exists. Then $\alpha - 1$ is not an upper bound, so some $n \in \mathbb{N}$ has $n > \alpha - 1$. But then $n + 1 > \alpha$ and $n + 1 \in \mathbb{N}$, contradicting that $\alpha$ is an upper bound of $\mathbb{N}$.(ii) Apply (i) to $x = 1/y$: some $n > 1/y$, so $1/n < y$. $\blacksquare$

This property looks obvious, yet the proof needs completeness: there are ordered fields (for example suitably ordered fields of rational functions) with "infinitely large" elements, where it fails.

Density of the rationals

Theorem (density of $\mathbb{Q}$). For all real $a < b$ there is a rational $r$ with $a < r < b$.

Proof. By the Archimedean property choose $n \in \mathbb{N}$ with $1/n < b - a$. Let $m$ be the least integer with $m > na$ (it exists by the Archimedean property and well-ordering), so $m - 1 \le na < m$. Then $a < m/n$, and

$$m \le na + 1 < na + n(b - a) = nb,$$

using $1 < n(b - a)$. So $a < m/n < b$. $\blacksquare$

The idea: steps of size $1/n$ are shorter than the interval, so one of the points $m/n$ must land inside.

Corollary. The irrationals are dense too: between any $a < b$ there is an irrational number. (Find a rational $r \ne 0$ with $a/\sqrt{2} < r < b/\sqrt{2}$; then $r\sqrt{2}$ is irrational and lies in $(a, b)$.)

Existence of the square root of 2

Theorem. There is $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ with $\alpha^2 = 2$.

Proof sketch. Let $T = \{t \in \mathbb{R} : t^2 < 2\}$. It is nonempty ($1 \in T$) and bounded above by $2$ (if $t > 2$ then $t^2 > 4$). Let $\alpha = \sup T$; note $\alpha \ge 1$. Show that the other two cases of trichotomy are impossible.

Case $\alpha^2 < 2$. For $n \in \mathbb{N}$,

$$\left(\alpha + \tfrac{1}{n}\right)^2 = \alpha^2 + \frac{2\alpha}{n} + \frac{1}{n^2} \le \alpha^2 + \frac{2\alpha + 1}{n}.$$

Choose $n$ with $\frac{1}{n} < \frac{2 - \alpha^2}{2\alpha + 1}$ (Archimedean property). Then $(\alpha + 1/n)^2 < 2$, so $\alpha + 1/n \in T$, contradicting that $\alpha$ is an upper bound.

Case $\alpha^2 > 2$. Now $\left(\alpha - \tfrac{1}{n}\right)^2 > \alpha^2 - \frac{2\alpha}{n}$. Choose $n$ with $\frac{1}{n} < \frac{\alpha^2 - 2}{2\alpha}$; then $(\alpha - 1/n)^2 > 2$, and $\alpha - 1/n > 0$. Any $t \in T$ with $t > \alpha - 1/n > 0$ would have $t^2 > (\alpha - 1/n)^2 > 2$, which is impossible. So $\alpha - 1/n$ is an upper bound of $T$ smaller than $\alpha$ — a contradiction.

So $\alpha^2 = 2$. $\blacksquare$

The same argument, with $\mathbb{Q}$ in place of $\mathbb{R}$, is the proof that $\{r \in \mathbb{Q} : r^2 < 2\}$ has no supremum in $\mathbb{Q}$: its supremum would have to square to $2$. With more algebra it gives $n$-th roots of every positive real.

The Nested Interval Property

Theorem (Nested Interval Property). For each $n \in \mathbb{N}$ let $In = [an, bn]$ be a closed bounded interval, with $I1 \supseteq I2 \supseteq I3 \supseteq \cdots$. Then $\bigcap{n=1}^\infty In \ne \emptyset$.

Proof. Nesting means $a1 \le a2 \le \cdots$ and $\cdots \le b2 \le b1$, and $an \le bm$ for all $n, m$ (with $k = \max(n,m)$: $an \le ak \le bk \le bm$). So the set $A = \{an : n \in \mathbb{N}\}$ is nonempty and bounded above by every $bm$. Let $x = \sup A$. Then $an \le x$ for all $n$ (upper bound) and $x \le bm$ for all $m$ (each $bm$ is an upper bound and $x$ is the least). So $x \in In$ for every $n$. $\blacksquare$

If in addition the lengths $bn - an$ shrink to $0$, the intersection is exactly one point.

> Watch out: both hypotheses are needed. The open intervals $(0, 1/n)$ are nested but their intersection is empty (any $x > 0$ is excluded once $1/n < x$). The closed but unbounded intervals $[n, \infty)$ are nested with empty intersection too. And in $\mathbb{Q}$ the property fails: intervals of rationals closing in on $\sqrt{2}$ have no common rational point.

Together with the Archimedean property, the Nested Interval Property is equivalent to the Axiom of Completeness. It is the version used in bisection arguments (Bolzano–Weierstrass in Real Analysis 01, the Intermediate Value Theorem in Real Analysis 02).

Chapter 5 — Countable and uncountable sets

Completeness makes $\mathbb{R}$ enormously larger than $\mathbb{Q}$, even though both are dense. Cardinality makes "larger" precise.

Terminology

- Function $f : A \to B$ — a rule assigning to each $a \in A$ one element $f(a) \in B$.- One-to-one (injective) — $f(a1) = f(a2)$ implies $a1 = a2$.- Onto (surjective) — every $b \in B$ equals $f(a)$ for some $a$.- Bijection — one-to-one and onto. $A$ and $B$ have the same cardinality, written $A \sim B$, if a bijection $A \to B$ exists.- Countable — $A \sim \mathbb{N}$ (an infinite list $a1, a2, a3, \dots$ with every element appearing exactly once). Uncountable — infinite and not countable.

The rationals are countable

First, $\mathbb{Z}$ is countable: the list $0, 1, -1, 2, -2, 3, -3, \dots$ is the bijection $f : \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{Z}$ with $f(n) = (n-1)/2$ for odd $n$ and $f(n) = -n/2$ for even $n$.

Theorem. $\mathbb{Q}$ is countable.

Proof. For each $n \in \mathbb{N}$ let $An$ be the set of rationals that can be written as $\pm p/q$ in lowest terms with integers $p \ge 0$, $q \ge 1$ and $p + q = n$. For example $A1 = \{0\}$, $A2 = \{1, -1\}$, $A3 = \{1/2, -1/2, 2, -2\}$. Each $An$ is finite, the $An$ are disjoint, and every rational lies in exactly one of them. List $A1$, then $A2$, then $A3$, and so on. This list contains every rational exactly once, so it is a bijection with $\mathbb{N}$. $\blacksquare$

The reals are uncountable

Theorem (Cantor, 1874; diagonal argument 1891). The interval $(0, 1)$, and hence $\mathbb{R}$, is uncountable.

Proof. Every $x \in (0,1)$ has a decimal expansion $x = 0.d1 d2 d3 \ldots$. It is unique if expansions ending in an infinite string of $9$s are excluded (so $0.4999\ldots$ is written $0.5000\ldots$). Suppose $f : \mathbb{N} \to (0,1)$ were onto, and write

$$\begin{aligned}f(1) &= 0.\,a{11}\, a{12}\, a{13}\, a{14} \ldots \\f(2) &= 0.\,a{21}\, a{22}\, a{23}\, a{24} \ldots \\f(3) &= 0.\,a{31}\, a{32}\, a{33}\, a{34} \ldots \\&\ \ \vdots\end{aligned}$$

Define $x = 0.b1 b2 b3 \ldots$ by $bn = 2$ if $a{nn} \ne 2$ and $bn = 3$ if $a{nn} = 2$. This $x$ lies in $(0,1)$, its expansion has no $9$s (so it is the unique allowed one), and it differs from $f(n)$ in the $n$-th digit for every $n$. So $x \ne f(n)$ for all $n$, and $f$ is not onto. $\blacksquare$

Since $(0,1) \sim \mathbb{R}$ (for example via $x \mapsto \tan(\pi x - \pi/2)$), $\mathbb{R}$ is uncountable. Consequences:

- The irrationals $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ are uncountable (if they were countable, $\mathbb{R}$ would be a union of two countable sets, which is countable).- Almost all real numbers are irrational, even though the rationals are dense. Density is about where points are; cardinality is about how many.

> Common pitfall: "the diagonal number might equal some $f(n)$ with a different expansion". That is exactly why the proof uses the digits $2$ and $3$ only and excludes trailing $9$s: the number $x$ has only one decimal expansion, so differing in a digit really means being a different number.

The Cantor set

Start with $C0 = [0,1]$. Remove the open middle third to get $C1 = [0, \tfrac13] \cup [\tfrac23, 1]$. Remove the open middle third of each piece to get $C2$, a union of $4$ intervals of length $\tfrac19$. In general $Cn$ is a union of $2^n$ closed intervals of length $3^{-n}$. The Cantor set is $C = \bigcap{n=0}^\infty Cn$.

- Length removed: at step $k$ you remove $2^{k-1}$ intervals of length $3^{-k}$, so the total is $\sum{k=1}^\infty 2^{k-1}/3^k = 1$. The Cantor set has "length zero".- Many points survive: $0$, $1$, $\tfrac13$, $\tfrac23$ and every endpoint stay forever. So does $\tfrac14$, which is never an endpoint: in base $3$, $\tfrac14 = 0.020202\ldots3$, since $\sum{k=1}^\infty 2/9^k = 2/8 = 1/4$.- Uncountable: $x \in C$ exactly when $x$ has a base-$3$ expansion using only the digits $0$ and $2$. Replacing each $2$ by $1$ and reading the result in base $2$ maps $C$ onto $[0,1]$, so $C$ is uncountable.

The Cantor set is the standard example of a set that is "small" in length but "large" in cardinality. It returns in Real Analysis 02 as a closed, compact set that contains no interval.

Practice Exercises

1. Write the negation of: "for every $M > 0$ there is $N \in \mathbb{N}$ such that for all $n \ge N$, $an > M$" (the definition of $an \to \infty$).

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

"There is $M > 0$ such that for every $N \in \mathbb{N}$ there is $n \ge N$ with $an \le M$." In words: there is a level $M$ at or below which infinitely many terms stay.

</details>

2. Prove by induction that $1 + 2 + \cdots + n = \frac{n(n+1)}{2}$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Base: $1 = \frac{1 \cdot 2}{2}$. Step: if $1 + \cdots + n = \frac{n(n+1)}{2}$, then $1 + \cdots + n + (n + 1) = \frac{n(n+1)}{2} + (n+1) = \frac{(n+1)(n+2)}{2}$, which is the formula for $n + 1$.

</details>

3. Prove that $\sqrt{3}$ is irrational.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

First, if $3$ divides $n^2$ then $3$ divides $n$: otherwise $n = 3k \pm 1$ and $n^2 = 9k^2 \pm 6k + 1$ leaves remainder $1$ on division by $3$. Now suppose $\sqrt{3} = p/q$ in lowest terms. Then $p^2 = 3q^2$, so $3$ divides $p$; write $p = 3k$. Then $9k^2 = 3q^2$, so $q^2 = 3k^2$ and $3$ divides $q$. Both divisible by $3$ contradicts lowest terms.

</details>

4. Find $\sup A$ and $\inf A$ for $A = \left\{ \frac{n}{n+1} : n \in \mathbb{N} \right\}$ and prove your answer for the supremum.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

$\inf A = \frac12$ (the minimum, at $n = 1$, since $\frac{n}{n+1} = 1 - \frac{1}{n+1}$ increases with $n$). $\sup A = 1$: it is an upper bound since $\frac{n}{n+1} < 1$. Given $\varepsilon > 0$, choose $n$ with $\frac{1}{n+1} < \varepsilon$ (Archimedean); then $\frac{n}{n+1} = 1 - \frac{1}{n+1} > 1 - \varepsilon$. By the $\varepsilon$-characterization, $\sup A = 1$. It is not attained.

</details>

5. Let $A, B$ be nonempty and bounded above, and $A + B = \{a + b : a \in A, b \in B\}$. Prove $\sup(A + B) = \sup A + \sup B$.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Let $s = \sup A$, $t = \sup B$. For $a \in A$, $b \in B$: $a + b \le s + t$, so $s + t$ is an upper bound. Given $\varepsilon > 0$, pick $a > s - \varepsilon/2$ and $b > t - \varepsilon/2$; then $a + b > (s + t) - \varepsilon$. By the $\varepsilon$-characterization, $\sup(A + B) = s + t$.

</details>

6. Show that the intervals $In = (0, 1/n]$ are nested but have empty intersection. Which hypothesis of the Nested Interval Property fails?

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

$1/(n+1) < 1/n$ gives $I{n+1} \subseteq In$. If $x \le 0$, $x \notin I1$. If $x > 0$, choose $n$ with $1/n < x$ (Archimedean); then $x \notin In$. So the intersection is empty. The intervals are not closed (the left endpoint $0$ is missing).

</details>

7. Show that the set of all finite subsets of $\mathbb{N}$ is countable, but the set $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})$ of all subsets of $\mathbb{N}$ is not.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Finite subsets: for each $n$, the subsets of $\{1, \dots, n\}$ form a finite collection ($2^n$ sets), and every finite subset of $\mathbb{N}$ belongs to one of these collections. List the first collection, then the new sets of the second, and so on; this lists every finite subset exactly once. All subsets: suppose $f : \mathbb{N} \to \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})$ were onto. Let $B = \{n : n \notin f(n)\}$. If $B = f(m)$, then $m \in B \iff m \notin f(m) = B$, a contradiction. (This is Cantor's theorem, the same diagonal idea.)

</details>

Cheat-sheet

| Idea | Statement | Used for ||------|-----------|----------|| Negation | flip each quantifier, negate the final condition | "does not converge", "not continuous" || Triangle inequality | $\lvert x + y\rvert \le \lvert x\rvert + \lvert y\rvert$ | splitting every $\varepsilon$ estimate || Reverse triangle | $\big\lvert \lvert x\rvert - \lvert y\rvert\big\rvert \le \lvert x - y\rvert$ | lower bounds on $\lvert an \rvert$ || Equality lemma | $a = b$ iff $\lvert a - b\rvert < \varepsilon$ for all $\varepsilon > 0$ | proving limits are unique || Supremum | least upper bound; may not be in the set | defining limits, integrals || $\varepsilon$-test | upper bound $s$, and some $a > s - \varepsilon$ for each $\varepsilon$ | every sup computation || Completeness | nonempty + bounded above gives a sup in $\mathbb{R}$ | the one axiom $\mathbb{Q}$ lacks || Archimedean | $\mathbb{N}$ unbounded; $1/n < y$ for some $n$ | choosing $N$ in limit proofs || Density | a rational (and an irrational) in every $(a,b)$ | Dirichlet function, approximation || Nested intervals | closed, bounded, nested: nonempty intersection | bisection proofs || Countable | $\mathbb{N}$, $\mathbb{Z}$, $\mathbb{Q}$ | listing arguments || Uncountable | $\mathbb{R}$, $(0,1)$, irrationals, Cantor set | diagonal argument |

What's next

Real Analysis 01 uses the Axiom of Completeness to study sequences and series: the $\varepsilon$–$N$ definition of a limit with an explicit $N$, the Monotone Convergence Theorem (a supremum in disguise), Bolzano–Weierstrass (nested intervals in disguise), Cauchy sequences, and the convergence tests for infinite series, including why rearranging a series can change its sum.

Bartle and Donald R. Sherbert, Introduction to Real Analysis, 4th edition, Wiley, 2011 — Chapters 1–2.- Terence Tao, Analysis I, 4th edition, Springer (Texts and Readings in Mathematics), 2022 — Chapters 2–5 (from the natural numbers to the construction of $\mathbb{R}$).- Michael Spivak, Calculus, 4th edition, Publish or Perish, 2008 — Chapters 1–2 and 8 (least upper bounds).- MIT OpenCourseWare, 18.100A Real Analysis, Fall 2020 (Casey Rodriguez), lecture notes: https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-100a-real-analysis-fall-2020/

12:T87df,The workloads run, they are scheduled sensibly, and they survive a rollout. What remains is everything around them: how external traffic reaches the cluster, how Pods are prevented from talking to each other, how you find out what is wrong at three in the morning, who is allowed to do what, and how manifests get from a Git repository into a cluster without a human running  from a laptop. This is the last post of the base course, and it is the one that turns a working cluster into an operable platform.

> Series map: 00 Containers from First Principles → 01 Images and Dockerfiles → 02 Networking, Volumes and Compose → 03 Registries, Supply Chain and Security → 04 Kubernetes Architecture and Core Objects → 05 Config, Storage and Stateful Workloads → 06 Scheduling, Resources, Probes and Rollouts → 07 Ingress, Observability, RBAC and GitOps (this post — final base post).

Learning Outcome

- Route external HTTP traffic, terminate TLS, and automate certificates with cert-manager.- State the current status of the Gateway API and decide whether to start there instead of Ingress.- Write a default-deny NetworkPolicy, and judge honestly whether a service mesh is worth its cost.- Assemble the metrics/logs/traces triad on Kubernetes and alert on symptoms rather than causes.- Debug a broken Pod with describe, previous logs, events and ephemeral debug containers.- Design least-privilege RBAC, apply Pod Security Admission, and enforce policy with an admission controller.- Choose between Helm and Kustomize, run delivery with Argo CD or Flux, and control managed-cluster cost.

Chapter 1 — Getting traffic in

Terminology

- Ingress — a built-in API object describing HTTP/HTTPS routing rules (host, path, backend Service, TLS).- Ingress controller — the component that actually implements those rules. Kubernetes ships the API, not an implementation.- Gateway API — the newer, role-oriented, extensible routing API that supersedes Ingress. Installed as CRDs, not built in.- TLS termination — decrypting HTTPS at the edge so backends receive plain HTTP.- ACME — the protocol (used by Let's Encrypt) for automated certificate issuance.

Ingress

A  Service per application means one cloud load balancer per application, each with its own IP, its own bill and its own certificate. Ingress collapses that into one entry point that routes by hostname and path.

The controller watches Ingress objects and reconfigures its own proxy. Note what the API does not cover: rate limiting, header rewriting, request mirroring, timeouts, retries, authentication. Every controller exposes those through vendor-specific annotations, which is precisely why Ingress manifests are not portable between controllers and why the Gateway API exists.

The ingress controller landscape, as of 2026

This changed materially and you need the current picture. The long-time default, ingress-nginx, was retired in March 2026: maintenance ceased, the repository was archived, and the planned successor project (InGate) was abandoned before reaching maturity. The Kubernetes SIG Network and Security Response Committee guidance is to migrate to the Gateway API or to another actively maintained controller.

| Option | Notes ||--------|-------|| Gateway API with a conformant implementation (Envoy Gateway, Traefik, Istio, Cilium, kgateway, cloud-native gateways) | the recommended direction for new clusters || Traefik | actively maintained, supports both Ingress and Gateway API, good developer ergonomics || HAProxy / F5 NGINX (commercial) / Envoy-based controllers | maintained Ingress implementations if you must stay on the Ingress API || Cloud-native — AWS Load Balancer Controller, GKE Ingress, AGIC | provisions the cloud's own L7 load balancer; least to operate, most vendor-coupled || ingress-nginx | retired — running it means running unmaintained, unpatched edge software |

If you are on ingress-nginx today, treat migration as a scheduled project, not an emergency: inventory your annotations first (that is where the work is), then use the  conversion tool as a starting point and review everything it emits.

TLS with cert-manager

cert-manager is a controller that turns a certificate request into a Kubernetes Secret and keeps it renewed.

With the  annotation on an Ingress, cert-manager notices the  block, solves the ACME challenge, writes , and renews it automatically before expiry.

> Common pitfall: developing against  and burning through Let's Encrypt's rate limits in an afternoon. Use the ACME staging endpoint until the whole chain works, then switch. The staging certificate will not validate in a browser — that is the point.

The Gateway API

Gateway API replaces one overloaded object with a set of role-separated ones:

| Resource | Owned by | Purpose ||----------|----------|---------|| GatewayClass | infrastructure provider | which implementation, cluster-scoped || Gateway | platform / cluster operator | a listener: ports, protocols, TLS, which namespaces may attach || HTTPRoute / GRPCRoute / TLSRoute / TCPRoute | application team | the routing rules themselves |

Current status, stated plainly. Gateway API is a set of CRDs versioned independently of Kubernetes and installed separately. Its Standard (GA) channel has carried GatewayClass, Gateway and HTTPRoute since v1.0 in late 2023, with GRPCRoute following, and v1.5 (early 2026) promoted a further six features from Experimental to Standard. Several conformant implementations exist. The Ingress API remains GA and supported but is feature-frozen — all active development happens in Gateway API. Meanwhile the most widely deployed Ingress controller has been retired.

The practical reading: new clusters should start on the Gateway API; existing Ingress deployments keep working and should be migrated deliberately, prioritising any cluster still running ingress-nginx. Check the conformance report for your chosen implementation rather than assuming a feature is available — extended features are genuinely optional.

Chapter 2 — East-west traffic: CNI, NetworkPolicy and meshes

The CNI model

Kubernetes does not implement Pod networking. It defines a contract — CNI (Container Network Interface) — and requires that every Pod gets its own IP, that any Pod can reach any other Pod without NAT, and that nodes can reach all Pods. A plugin (Calico, Cilium, Flannel, AWS VPC CNI, Azure CNI, Weave) fulfils it, with very different implementations: overlay tunnels (VXLAN, Geneve), native routing (BGP), or cloud-native IP allocation from the VPC.

Two consequences: the default posture is completely flat — every Pod in every namespace can reach every other Pod, on every port — and NetworkPolicy is enforced by the CNI plugin, not by Kubernetes. A NetworkPolicy applied on a cluster whose plugin does not implement it is silently accepted and does nothing at all. Verify enforcement before you rely on it.

Default-deny, then open what is needed

Rules to internalise:

- Policies are additive whitelists. A Pod selected by any policy denies everything not explicitly allowed by some policy that selects it. A Pod selected by no policy allows everything.-  with both  and no rules is the idiomatic default-deny.- Always allow DNS egress explicitly. Forgetting it is the single most common NetworkPolicy incident: name resolution fails, every outbound call breaks, and the errors point everywhere except the network policy.- Policies are namespace-scoped and select by label. Cross-namespace rules need ; the automatic  label makes that convenient.- Standard NetworkPolicy is L3/L4 only — IPs, ports, protocols. It cannot express "GET but not DELETE" or "only with a valid identity". CNI-specific CRDs (Cilium's , Calico's ) add L7 rules, and the emerging  API adds cluster-wide rules that individual namespaces cannot override.

> Trick: roll out default-deny in audit mode first if your CNI supports it, or apply the deny policy to one low-risk namespace and watch the errors. Applying default-deny to a busy production namespace on a Friday is a well-documented way to spend a weekend.

Service mesh, honestly

A service mesh (Istio, Linkerd, Cilium's mesh mode) puts a proxy in the path of every Pod-to-Pod call — historically a sidecar, increasingly a per-node or ambient component. In exchange for that you get automatic mTLS between all workloads, L7 traffic control (retries, timeouts, circuit breaking, canary splitting by header or weight), uniform golden-signal telemetry for every service without touching application code, and identity-based authorisation policy rather than IP-based.

The costs are real and routinely understated: an extra network hop and a few milliseconds on every call; noticeable CPU and memory per Pod for sidecar deployments; a substantial new control plane to upgrade in lockstep with the cluster; failure modes that are genuinely hard to debug because the proxy is now between you and every symptom; and a body of concepts (VirtualService, DestinationRule, PeerAuthentication, or their equivalents) the whole team must learn. Most clusters do not need one. If you have a handful of services, NetworkPolicy plus TLS at the ingress plus a decent HTTP client library covers the same ground for a fraction of the operational cost. Reach for a mesh when you have a real, specific driver — a compliance requirement for encryption in transit everywhere, dozens of teams needing uniform traffic policy, or progressive delivery you cannot express any other way — and when you have someone who will own it. Linkerd is the smaller, simpler option; Istio ambient mode and Cilium remove the per-Pod sidecar cost; a mesh chosen because it was on a conference slide is a liability.

Chapter 3 — Observability

The triad

| Signal | Answers | Typical stack ||--------|---------|---------------|| Metrics | "is something wrong, and how wrong?" — cheap, aggregatable, alertable | Prometheus, Thanos/Mimir/Cortex, cloud-native metrics || Logs | "what exactly happened in this request?" — high detail, high volume | Fluent Bit or Vector → Loki, OpenSearch, or a managed backend || Traces | "where did the latency go across N services?" | OpenTelemetry → Jaeger, Tempo, or a vendor |

Metrics tell you that; traces tell you where; logs tell you why. You need all three, in that order of priority for a small team.

Prometheus and kube-state-metrics

The  chart is the conventional starting point. It brings four things that are easy to confuse:

- Prometheus — scrapes and stores time series; discovers targets from the Kubernetes API.- node-exporter (DaemonSet) — node metrics: CPU, memory, disk, filesystem, network.- cAdvisor (inside the kubelet) — container resource metrics: , .- kube-state-metrics — object state metrics generated from the API server: , , . Not resource usage — the declared and observed state of objects.

That last distinction matters constantly: "is the container using too much memory" is cAdvisor; "have the desired replicas been available for ten minutes" is kube-state-metrics. Note also that metrics-server is a separate, minimal component that exists only to serve  and the HPA — it is not a monitoring system and does not store history.

Alert on symptoms, not causes

The discipline that separates a usable on-call rotation from an ignored one: page on what users experience; make everything else a dashboard or a ticket.

Cause-based alerts ("CPU above 80 %", "a Pod restarted", "disk 70 % full", "replica count below desired") fire constantly, are usually harmless, and train people to ignore the pager. Symptom-based alerts describe a violated expectation:

- Error rate on  exceeded 1 % of requests over 5 minutes.- p99 latency for checkout exceeded 2 s over 10 minutes.- The nightly export CronJob has not produced a successful Job in 26 hours.- The service is consuming its error budget fast enough to exhaust the month's allowance in under a day (multi-window, multi-burn-rate SLO alerting).

Useful frameworks: RED (Rate, Errors, Duration) for request-driven services, USE (Utilisation, Saturation, Errors) for resources, and the four golden signals (latency, traffic, errors, saturation). A small number of genuinely paging alerts beats a hundred noisy ones.

A short list of Kubernetes-specific conditions genuinely worth paging on: a Deployment with zero available replicas;  persisting beyond a few minutes; Pods  for more than N minutes (capacity); a node  beyond the eviction timeout; PersistentVolume nearly full; certificate expiring within a week; API server error rate or latency elevated.

Logging when Pods are ephemeral

reads from the node's log files for a currently existing Pod. Delete the Pod — as every rollout, eviction and node scale-down does — and the logs are gone.  reaches exactly one container generation back, and rotation discards the rest. That is not a logging strategy.

The standard architecture is a node-level agent (Fluent Bit or Vector as a DaemonSet) that tails , enriches each line with Kubernetes metadata (namespace, Pod, container, labels) from the API, and ships it to a store — Loki, OpenSearch, or a managed service. Requirements that pay for themselves:

- Log to stdout/stderr as structured JSON. Writing to a file inside the container means the agent never sees it. Unstructured text means you cannot query by field.- Carry the trace ID in every line. It is what joins the three signals.- Set retention by value, not by default. Logs are usually the largest line in an observability bill; 7 days hot plus 90 days in cheap object storage is a common shape.- Do not log secrets or personal data. Once shipped, it is in someone else's index and in their backups.

OpenTelemetry

OpenTelemetry is the vendor-neutral standard for producing and shipping all three signals: one set of APIs and SDKs, one wire protocol (OTLP), and the OpenTelemetry Collector as a pipeline of receivers, processors and exporters. On Kubernetes it is typically deployed twice — as a DaemonSet for node-local collection and enrichment, and as a Deployment (gateway) for aggregation, sampling and fan-out to backends. The OpenTelemetry Operator adds auto-instrumentation by injecting language agents into Pods via an annotation, which gets you traces from a Java, Python, Node or .NET service without editing its code.

The reason to standardise on it is portability: instrument once, and changing backends becomes a Collector configuration change instead of a re-instrumentation project. For a full worked treatment of OpenTelemetry — spans, context propagation, sampling strategy, collector configuration and correlating traces with database telemetry — see .

Debugging a broken Pod

An ordered toolkit, roughly cheapest first:

When the image is distroless or scratch and has no shell — as a hardened image should be (Post 03) — use an ephemeral debug container, GA since Kubernetes 1.25:

> Common pitfall: reaching for  and finding no shell, then rebuilding the image with a shell "for debugging" and shipping that to production. Ephemeral containers exist precisely so that production images can stay minimal. Ensure your RBAC permits  for the people who need it — and only them.

Chapter 4 — Security: RBAC, Pod Security and admission control

RBAC

Four object types, one simple model.

- Role — a set of permissions, namespaced.- ClusterRole — the same, but cluster-scoped; also the only way to grant access to cluster-scoped resources (nodes, PVs, CRDs) or to non-resource URLs.- RoleBinding — grants a Role or a ClusterRole to subjects within one namespace.- ClusterRoleBinding — grants a ClusterRole across the whole cluster.

Binding a ClusterRole with a RoleBinding is the most useful idiom: define the permission set once, grant it namespace by namespace.

Verbs: , , , , , , , , plus ,  and . RBAC is purely additive — there is no deny rule. If you need denial, that is admission control's job.

Three permissions deserve special caution, because each is effectively cluster-admin in disguise:  in a namespace whose ServiceAccounts are privileged (you can mount any Secret and run as any identity);  or  on roles (you can grant yourself more); and  at cluster scope (you can read every credential in the cluster).

ServiceAccounts and their tokens

Every Pod runs as a ServiceAccount —  in its namespace unless told otherwise, and  should have no permissions at all. Give each workload its own:

Token handling changed and the modern behaviour matters. Since Kubernetes 1.24, creating a ServiceAccount no longer auto-creates a non-expiring Secret token. Pods receive a projected, time-bound, audience-scoped token that the kubelet rotates automatically. For an out-of-cluster consumer, mint one explicitly with  rather than creating a long-lived Secret. Long-lived static tokens are exactly the credential that ends up in a CI variable and never gets rotated.

On managed clusters, bind ServiceAccounts to cloud identity (IRSA on EKS, Workload Identity on GKE and AKS) so Pods obtain short-lived cloud credentials without any stored secret.

The least-privilege audit

A concrete, repeatable procedure:

Then: remove every wildcard you cannot justify, replace human cluster-admin with time-bound elevation, delete unused bindings, and re-run quarterly. Tools such as ,  and  automate the reporting.

Pod Security Admission

PodSecurityPolicy was deprecated in 1.21 and removed in Kubernetes 1.25. Its replacement, Pod Security Admission, is a built-in admission controller — stable since 1.25 — configured with namespace labels rather than cluster-wide policy objects.

Three levels:  (no restrictions),  (blocks known privilege escalations: host namespaces, privileged containers, most host paths), and  (baseline plus hardening: run as non-root, drop all capabilities, seccomp , no privilege escalation, restricted volume types). Three modes:  (reject),  (record in the audit log),  (return a warning to the client).

The migration path is exactly that shape: enforce , audit and warn on , fix what the warnings reveal, then raise enforcement. Pin  so a cluster upgrade cannot tighten policy underneath you unannounced.

PSA is deliberately limited — it checks Pod security context fields and nothing else. It cannot say "images must come from our registry" or "every Deployment must set resource requests". That needs a policy engine.

Admission control and policy engines

Every request to the API server passes through authentication, then authorisation (RBAC), then admission: first mutating admission (which can modify the object — inject sidecars, set defaults), then schema validation of the mutated object, then validating admission (which can only accept or reject), then persistence.

You extend it three ways:

- MutatingAdmissionWebhook — an HTTPS service you run that patches objects. Powerful, and a liability: if it is down and , the API server rejects the requests it intercepts. Always scope webhooks narrowly with  and , and exclude .- ValidatingAdmissionPolicy — in-tree validation expressed in CEL, evaluated by the API server itself, GA since 1.30. No webhook to run or fail. The right first choice for simple rules.- Policy engines — Kyverno (policies are Kubernetes resources written in YAML; validate, mutate, generate and verify image signatures; a gentle learning curve) or OPA Gatekeeper (constraint templates written in Rego; more expressive, steeper curve, shared with non-Kubernetes OPA deployments).

Policies worth having early: require resource requests; require images from an approved registry and by digest (Post 03); forbid  tags; require ; require ownership and cost-centre labels; forbid  and  outside named namespaces. Run everything in Audit mode first — enforcing a policy that most existing workloads violate blocks deploys immediately.

Chapter 5 — Packaging and delivery

Helm

Helm is a package manager: a chart is a directory of Go-templated manifests plus a  of defaults; installing it produces a release whose state Helm stores in a Secret in the cluster.

Helm's strengths are real: one command installs a complex third-party system, versioned and parameterised; conditionals and loops express genuine variation; the ecosystem of charts is enormous.

The sharp edges are equally real and worth stating before you commit:

- It is text templating over YAML. Indentation is your problem;  and  are load-bearing; a template that produces valid text can produce invalid YAML. Error messages point at rendered lines you never wrote.- Charts become unreadable. A generic chart with forty conditionals serving five environments is harder to reason about than five explicit manifests.- Release state lives in the cluster, not in Git. Two operators, two laptops, two versions of the chart, and the state is whatever the last  wrote.-  is not optional. Without it a failed upgrade leaves the release half-applied. Also set  deliberately.- CRDs in  are installed but never upgraded or deleted by Helm. Chart upgrades that change a CRD require manual work; this surprises everyone once.- Values sprawl. Deeply nested values files across environments drift, and nothing type-checks them. Schema-validate with .

Kustomize

Kustomize takes the opposite approach: no templating language, only overlays that patch plain YAML. It is built into  ().

Every file is valid Kubernetes YAML that your editor, linter and  all understand. The  hash-suffix behaviour implements the rollout-on-config-change pattern from Post 05 for free. The limits: no conditionals or loops, so genuinely divergent environments mean duplication; deep patch hierarchies get hard to follow; and it does not package or distribute anything.

| | Helm | Kustomize ||---|------|-----------|| Mechanism | Go templating + values | patch plain YAML || Best at | distributing third-party software, wide parameterisation | your own manifests across a few environments || Readability | rendered output differs from source | source is the output || Distribution | repositories, versioned charts, OCI registries | Git directories || State | release history in-cluster | none — the output is applied || Common practice | use Helm for other people's software, Kustomize for your own (or Kustomize's  field for both) | |

GitOps

GitOps inverts the direction of deployment. Instead of CI pushing to the cluster with credentials, an agent inside the cluster pulls the desired state from Git and reconciles continuously. Git is the source of truth; the cluster converges on it.

Argo CD models this with an  CRD, a strong UI, sync waves and hooks for ordering, and an "app of apps" pattern for managing many. Flux is a set of focused controllers (source, kustomize, helm, image automation, notification) driven entirely by CRDs, with no UI of its own. Both do the same job well; Argo CD suits teams who want the dashboard, Flux suits teams who want everything in the API.

What you actually gain:

- Drift detection and correction.  means a manual  is reverted within seconds. The cluster cannot silently diverge from the repository.- The audit trail is the Git history. Who changed what, when, reviewed by whom, and how to revert — for free, in a system everyone already knows.- Rollback is . No special tooling, no remembering which Helm revision was good.- Disaster recovery becomes re-pointing an agent at the repository rather than reconstructing state from memory.

And the consequence that matters most: human access to the cluster should shrink once GitOps is in place. If every change arrives through a reviewed pull request, engineers do not need ,  or  in production. Reduce them to read plus ,  and  where justified, with break-glass elevation that is time-bound and alerted on. This is the point at which the RBAC work in Chapter 4 becomes genuinely easy — most people simply do not need write access any more.

Two caveats worth planning for: secrets still cannot be committed in plaintext, so pair GitOps with Sealed Secrets, SOPS, or the External Secrets Operator (Post 05); and image tags in Git must be updated by something — a CI step that commits the new tag, or an image-automation controller — otherwise "GitOps" quietly becomes "someone edits the tag by hand".

Chapter 6 — Managed Kubernetes and cost control

The managed offerings

EKS (AWS), GKE (Google) and AKS (Azure) all run and upgrade the control plane, manage etcd and its backups, and integrate with the cloud's IAM, load balancers and storage. What differs is the amount of remaining work: GKE Autopilot removes node management almost entirely; EKS gives you the most control and the most assembly; AKS sits between them. All charge for the control plane on a per-cluster-hour basis (with a free tier on some), and all charge normally for the worker nodes, load balancers, storage and data transfer — which is where the money actually goes. Check current pricing pages rather than trusting any figure quoted in a blog post, including this one.

The genuinely important decisions are the same everywhere: how many clusters (each has a control-plane cost and an operational cost — resist one per team until you have a reason), what your upgrade cadence is (Kubernetes releases roughly three times a year and support windows are finite), and whether your workloads can tolerate the node churn that spot instances and consolidation require.

Where the money goes, and what to do about it

| Lever | Action | Typical effect ||-------|--------|----------------|| Over-requested resources | right-size from measured usage (Post 06) | usually the single largest win; requests are often multiples of real usage || Idle nodes | Cluster Autoscaler scale-down or Karpenter consolidation | reclaims capacity nobody is using || On-demand everything | spot/preemptible for stateless and batch, with PDBs and graceful shutdown | large discount, in exchange for interruption handling || Oversized instance types | let Karpenter choose per workload rather than one node group for all | better bin-packing || Cross-AZ traffic | topology-aware routing; keep chatty pairs in one zone | inter-zone transfer is metered and adds up quietly || Orphaned resources | audit unbound PVCs, unused LoadBalancers, old snapshots, dangling images | recurring, always finds something || Logs and metrics | retention tiers; drop high-cardinality labels | observability bills can rival compute || Cluster count | consolidate with namespaces + RBAC + quotas instead of new clusters | removes whole control planes |

Make it visible and enforceable: OpenCost or Kubecost attribute spend to namespaces, labels and teams; a  and  per namespace stop one team consuming the cluster; and mandatory cost-centre labels (enforced by the Kyverno policy in Chapter 4) are what makes attribution possible at all. A monthly review of the top ten namespaces by cost against their requests-versus-usage ratio takes half an hour and consistently pays for itself.

Course base complete — what's next

That is the base course. Across eight posts you have gone from a process isolated by namespaces and cgroups on a single host to a multi-node platform with declarative delivery: 00-03 covered what a container actually is, how images are built and layered, networking and volumes and Compose, and image supply-chain security; 04 the reconciliation model, cluster components, Pods, Deployments, Services and DNS; 05 configuration and secrets, the storage stack from Volume to CSI, StatefulSets, DaemonSets, Jobs and CronJobs; 06 scheduling and placement, requests versus limits, QoS and eviction, probes, rollouts, disruption budgets, graceful shutdown and autoscaling; and 07 ingress and the Gateway API, NetworkPolicy, observability, RBAC and admission control, Helm and Kustomize, GitOps and cost.

That is enough to run production services on Kubernetes competently and to read the documentation for anything not covered here without getting lost. The most valuable next step is not another post — it is to operate something real, break it in a non-production cluster on purpose, and practise the failure drills in the exercises until they are boring.

This post covers how it behaves under pressure: which node a Pod lands on, how much CPU and memory it may consume, what happens when it wants more than it asked for, how Kubernetes decides whether it is healthy, and how a new version replaces an old one without dropping requests. Almost every self-inflicted Kubernetes outage — cascading restarts, mysterious 502s during deploys, nodes that evict the wrong workloads, autoscalers that oscillate — traces back to one of the settings in this post.

Learning Outcome

- Describe the scheduler's filter-then-score cycle and predict why a Pod is .- Choose correctly between nodeSelector, node affinity, pod anti-affinity, taints and topology spread constraints.- Explain the single most consequential idea in Kubernetes resource management: requests drive scheduling, limits drive enforcement.- Determine a Pod's QoS class, predict its eviction order, and recognise an OOMKill from exit code 137.- Argue coherently about CPU limits and CFS throttling, and set memory limits without causing restarts.- Configure liveness, readiness and startup probes so they cannot cause the outage they are meant to prevent.- Run a rolling update that neither drops traffic nor breaks quorum, using surge settings, PodDisruptionBudgets,  and grace periods.- Configure an HPA that converges instead of oscillating, and right-size resources from measured data.

Chapter 1 — How the scheduler works

Terminology

- Scheduling — assigning an unscheduled Pod to a node by writing . The kubelet on that node then starts it.- Filter (predicate) — a check that eliminates nodes a Pod cannot run on.- Score (priority) — a ranking of the surviving nodes by how good a fit they are.- Binding — persisting the chosen node back to the API server.- Feasible node — a node that survived every filter.

The two phases

For each Pod in the scheduling queue,  runs a scheduling cycle and then a binding cycle. The scheduling cycle has two halves:

1. Filter. Every node is tested against a series of predicates. Any failure removes the node from consideration:

- Does the node have enough allocatable CPU, memory and ephemeral storage to satisfy the Pod's requests?- Do the Pod's  and required node affinity match the node's labels?- Are the node's taints tolerated by the Pod?- Are the requested host ports free?- Can the Pod's volumes be attached here (zone, node affinity of the PV, attach limits)?- Do required pod affinity and anti-affinity rules hold?- Is the node  and not under ,  or ?

If zero nodes survive, the Pod stays  and the scheduler records an event naming the failing predicate — . That event is the answer to almost every "why is my Pod Pending" question.

2. Score. Each feasible node is scored 0-100 by several plugins and the totals are weighted:

-  — spread load (, the default) or pack it tight (, better for autoscaler-driven cost savings).-  — prefer nodes that already have the image cached.- , , ,  — the preferred (soft) forms of each rule.

The highest-scoring node wins; ties are broken at random. The scheduler then reserves the resources, runs any  plugins, and binds asynchronously.

Two properties matter operationally:

- The scheduler decides once, at admission. It does not move a running Pod because the cluster later became unbalanced. Rebalancing requires deleting Pods (the  project automates this).- The scheduler reasons about requests, never about actual usage. A node can be 95 % idle and still reject a Pod because its requests are fully committed — and it can be 100 % CPU-saturated and happily accept more Pods whose requests fit.

> Trick:  prints an "Allocated resources" table with requests and limits as a percentage of allocatable. That table, not , is what the scheduler sees.

Chapter 2 — Controlling placement

nodeSelector — the simple case

Exact-match labels only, hard requirement. Fine for small, unambiguous rules; it cannot express preferences, negation, or set membership.

Node affinity — the expressive case

Read the long field names literally.  is a filter;  is a score contribution.  is the important half: an already-running Pod is never evicted because the node's labels changed. There is no  — if you need eviction on a condition change, use a taint with .

Pod affinity and anti-affinity

These place Pods relative to other Pods, using a  to define what "together" means.

> Common pitfall: hard  on  caps your replica count at the node count. Scale to 10 replicas on a 6-node cluster and 4 Pods sit  forever. Use the preferred form, or topology spread constraints, unless the co-location really is unacceptable.

Pod affinity is also computationally expensive — the scheduler must evaluate every Pod in the matched topology for every candidate node. On large clusters, prefer topology spread constraints.

Taints and tolerations — the inverse

Affinity is a Pod attracting a node. A taint is a node repelling Pods. Only Pods with a matching toleration may land there.

| Effect | Meaning ||--------|---------||  | no new Pod without a toleration ||  | soft version — avoid if possible ||  | as , plus evict already-running Pods that do not tolerate it (optionally after ) |

Taints are how you reserve expensive hardware (GPU nodes, high-memory nodes) for the workloads that need it, how control-plane nodes stay free of user workloads, and how the node lifecycle controller cordons off unhealthy nodes —  and  are  taints applied automatically.

Note that a toleration does not attract. Tolerating the GPU taint lets a Pod run on GPU nodes; it does not make it prefer them. Pair a toleration with node affinity to get both halves.

Topology spread constraints — the modern way to spread

is the maximum permitted difference between the most-populated and least-populated domain. With  across three zones, nine replicas land 3/3/3 and ten land 4/3/3. This expresses "spread evenly" directly, instead of approximating it with anti-affinity, and it is far cheaper for the scheduler to evaluate.

The standard production pairing for a stateless service: a hard zone spread with  and a soft hostname spread. That survives an availability-zone loss and still schedules when a zone is temporarily full.

> Watch out:  combined with a cluster autoscaler can deadlock — the Pod is unschedulable, so the autoscaler adds a node, but the new node is in the wrong zone, so the Pod remains unschedulable. Verify that your node groups actually span the topology you are spreading over.

Chapter 3 — Requests, limits and QoS

The one sentence that matters

Requests drive scheduling. Limits drive enforcement.

- A request is a reservation. The scheduler subtracts it from the node's allocatable capacity and guarantees the Pod that much. It is also the number that decides whether the Pod fits at all.- A limit is a ceiling enforced at runtime by the kernel through cgroups. Exceed the CPU limit and you are throttled. Exceed the memory limit and you are killed.

Everything else in this chapter follows from that distinction.

CPU is compressible: exceeding your share means running slower. Memory is incompressible: there is no "slower", only "killed".

QoS classes

The kubelet assigns each Pod a class based purely on how its requests and limits are written:

| Class | Condition | Consequence ||-------|-----------|-------------|| Guaranteed | every container sets both CPU and memory, and request == limit for each | evicted last; eligible for exclusive CPUs under the static CPU manager policy || Burstable | at least one request is set, but not the Guaranteed pattern | evicted after BestEffort, in order of how far usage exceeds requests || BestEffort | no requests or limits anywhere in the Pod | evicted first, always |

Under node memory pressure the kubelet evicts in that order: BestEffort first, then Burstable Pods that are using more than they requested (ranked by the excess), then Guaranteed. The kernel's own OOM killer uses a parallel mechanism, , derived from the same classes.

> Common pitfall: BestEffort Pods look thrifty and are actually hazardous. They are the first thing evicted when a node fills up, they contribute nothing to autoscaler capacity calculations, and they can starve neighbours. Set requests on everything, including the small "unimportant" jobs.

CPU limits and CFS throttling

A CPU limit is implemented as a CFS quota: the cgroup is granted quota microseconds of CPU time per period (100 ms by default). A limit of  means 50 ms of CPU per 100 ms window. When the quota is exhausted, every thread in the cgroup is stopped until the next period begins.

This produces a failure mode that is invisible in average CPU graphs. An application averaging 30 % of its limit can still be throttled hard, because the work is not spread evenly: a request arrives, several threads wake, the quota is consumed in 15 ms, and the container sleeps for the remaining 85 ms. The result is tail latency in whole multiples of 100 ms while the dashboard shows a comfortable average.

Multithreaded runtimes make it worse. A JVM or Go program that sees 32 host CPUs will happily run 32 threads, burning the quota 32× faster than a single thread would.

Measure it — do not guess:

Sustained values above roughly 5-10 % on a latency-sensitive service are worth acting on. Confirm on your own workload rather than trusting any published threshold.

The genuine argument for omitting CPU limits

Set memory limits always; consider omitting CPU limits on latency-sensitive services.

The case for omitting: CPU is compressible and already fairly shared. When several containers on a node contend, the kernel divides CPU time in proportion to their requests (cgroup CPU shares) — so requests alone already provide isolation under contention. A limit adds nothing during contention and actively hurts when the node is idle, because it forbids a container from using spare capacity it could have had for free. Throttling a service that could have run faster, on a machine that is 60 % idle, is pure loss.

The case for keeping them: limits make behaviour reproducible and capacity predictable, they stop one runaway process from degrading its neighbours' latency (fair sharing is fair, but a busy neighbour still slows you down), some regulated or multi-tenant environments require hard ceilings, and Guaranteed QoS — which requires a CPU limit — is needed for exclusive CPU pinning.

A defensible policy: memory limit always set and equal to a measured peak plus headroom; CPU request set from measured usage; CPU limit omitted for latency-sensitive services, set generously (well above the request) for batch and untrusted workloads. Whatever you choose, also set  (Go) or the container-aware JVM flags so the runtime sizes its thread pools to the cgroup, not to the host.

OOMKill and exit code 137

Exceed the memory limit and the kernel's OOM killer terminates the process with . The container exits with 137 — the shell convention of , and  is SIGKILL.

Diagnosis:

- Exit 137 with  — the container exceeded its own memory limit. Raise the limit or fix the leak.- Exit 137 without  — something sent SIGKILL: usually a grace period expiring during shutdown (Chapter 5), or a node-level OOM.- Pod status  — the node ran out of memory and the kubelet chose this Pod by QoS ranking. Different problem: the node is over-committed.- Exit 143 is  (SIGTERM) — a normal, graceful termination.

Note also that a request far below the real working set is what makes node-level OOM possible in the first place: the scheduler packed the node believing the Pods were small.

Chapter 4 — Probes

Three probes, three completely different jobs. Confusing them is the most common cause of self-inflicted outages in Kubernetes.

| Probe | Question | Action on failure ||-------|----------|-------------------|| liveness | "Is this process wedged beyond recovery?" | kill and restart the container || readiness | "Should this Pod receive traffic right now?" | remove the Pod from Service endpoints (no restart) || startup | "Has the process finished booting?" | kill the container; while it runs, liveness and readiness are suspended |

The classic outage

A team writes one  endpoint that checks the database, and wires it to both the readiness and the liveness probe. The database has a 30-second hiccup.

1. Every replica's liveness probe fails.2. The kubelet kills and restarts every replica simultaneously.3. The database recovers — and is immediately hit by the full fleet reconnecting and re-warming caches at once.4. The probes fail again. Restart storm. . The blast radius of a 30-second dependency blip is now a total outage that outlives it by many minutes.

The rule that prevents it: a liveness probe must never test anything outside the process. If the dependency is down, restarting your container does not help — it makes recovery harder by discarding warm state and multiplying reconnection load.

- Liveness → shallow. "Is the event loop running, can I serve a trivial response?" Many mature services are better off with no liveness probe at all; a process that crashes on a fatal fault is restarted by the Pod restart policy anyway.- Readiness → may be deep. Failing readiness removes the Pod from load balancing and is reversible with no state loss. If every replica fails readiness the Service has no endpoints and clients get connection errors — bad, but strictly better than a restart storm, and it recovers the instant the dependency does.- Startup → the correct tool for slow-starting applications. Without it you must inflate  or  on liveness, which weakens liveness forever to accommodate one slow boot.

Timing parameters

| Field | Default | Guidance ||-------|---------|----------||  | 0 | prefer a startup probe over a large value here ||  | 10 | 5-10s for readiness, 10-20s for liveness ||  | 1 | raise it — 1 second is shorter than many healthy responses under load ||  | 3 | liveness:  should exceed your worst legitimate GC pause or lock wait ||  | 1 | must be 1 for liveness and startup ||  | inherited | can be overridden per probe for the liveness-triggered kill |

> Watch out: the default  is the quiet cause of many phantom restarts. Under load, a healthy service can take longer than a second to answer, and three such answers in a row become a restart.

Probe types are , ,  and  (the gRPC health-checking protocol, GA since 1.27). Prefer  or ;  probes fork a process on every check and are surprisingly expensive at scale.

Chapter 5 — Rollouts, disruption and graceful shutdown

Rolling updates

-  — the safe default. Capacity never dips; the rollout needs spare cluster capacity and is slightly slower.-  — for workloads that cannot be over-provisioned (a licence limit, a singleton lock). Capacity dips during the roll.-  is underused and valuable: it stops a rollout that passes readiness and then dies five seconds later from marching all the way through the fleet.-  marks the Deployment  when it stalls, which is what your alerting and your GitOps controller should watch. Note that it does not roll back automatically.

bounds how far back  can go, at the cost of keeping that many zero-replica ReplicaSets. Blue/green and true canary deployments need more than a Deployment provides — Argo Rollouts, Flagger, or a service mesh (Post 07).

Voluntary versus involuntary disruption

| Involuntary | Voluntary ||-------------|-----------|| node hardware failure, kernel panic |  for an upgrade || node OOM, kubelet eviction | cluster autoscaler removing an under-used node || spot / preemptible instance reclaim | a rolling update you triggered || a container OOMKilled | deleting a Pod by hand |

A PodDisruptionBudget constrains only the voluntary column. It works by making the Eviction API refuse an eviction that would violate the budget; anything that does not go through that API — a node catching fire — is unaffected.

Guidance:

- Use  for large stateless fleets — it scales with replica count without editing.- Use  for quorum systems (etcd, Zookeeper, a 3-member database): losing quorum during a routine node drain is a real and common outage.- A PDB that can never be satisfied blocks node drains indefinitely.  on a Deployment with  means no Pod may ever be evicted, and every cluster upgrade hangs. Always leave slack.- A PDB on a single-replica Deployment is a contradiction: you are asserting that the Pod is both critical and unreplicated.

Graceful shutdown and the endpoint-removal race

When a Pod is deleted, two things happen concurrently, and this is the subtle part:

Path B is a distributed propagation across every node in the cluster; path A is local and immediate. So a container can receive SIGTERM, stop accepting connections, and still be sent traffic for hundreds of milliseconds to several seconds afterwards, by proxies that have not yet heard the news. That is the source of the 502s and connection resets that appear during otherwise "zero-downtime" deploys.

The fix is to make the container keep serving for a moment after SIGTERM:

runs before SIGTERM and the grace period clock covers both, so the grace period must exceed the sleep plus the real drain time. The sequence you want:

1.  sleeps 5-15 s. The container is still healthy and still serving; meanwhile every proxy learns the Pod is gone.2. SIGTERM. The application stops accepting new connections, finishes in-flight requests, closes pools, flushes buffers, and exits.3. If it has not exited by  (default 30), SIGKILL — exit code 137, in-flight work lost.

Requirements that are easy to miss:

- The application must actually handle SIGTERM. Many do not, and rely on being killed. Check.- PID 1 must receive the signal. A container started via  makes the shell PID 1, and it does not forward signals. Use exec form (), an init such as , or  semantics. This was covered in Post 01 and it bites hardest here.- Set the grace period from measurement. A worker draining 5-minute jobs needs a grace period longer than 5 minutes, or every deploy kills work in flight.

> Trick: if you cannot add a  binary to a distroless image, use  against an endpoint that flips readiness to false and blocks, or add a  implementation to the image explicitly. The pattern matters more than the mechanism.

Chapter 6 — Autoscaling

HorizontalPodAutoscaler

The HPA changes  on a Deployment or StatefulSet to keep a metric near a target. Use  — v1 supported CPU only.

Essential facts:

- The algorithm is , with a tolerance band (10 % by default) so small deviations do not cause churn.-  is a percentage of the CPU request, not of the node or the limit. A Pod requesting  and using  is at 90 % utilisation regardless of how idle the node is. An HPA on a workload with no CPU request cannot work at all.-  makes the controller use the maximum recommendation over the window when scaling down (and the minimum when scaling up), which is what stops flapping. Asymmetric windows — scale up fast, scale down slowly — are almost always right.- Resource metrics need metrics-server installed. Custom and external metrics need an adapter (typically Prometheus Adapter or KEDA).- Never point an HPA and a VPA at the same metric on the same workload. They fight: the VPA raises the request, which lowers utilisation, which makes the HPA scale in, which raises per-Pod load, which makes the VPA raise the request again.- The HPA cannot scale below 1 without the alpha/beta scale-to-zero feature; KEDA is the usual answer for event-driven scale-to-zero.

VerticalPodAutoscaler

The VPA recommends — and optionally applies — better CPU and memory requests, learned from observed usage. Modes:  (recommend only),  (apply at Pod creation), / (evict and recreate Pods to apply new values).

mode is genuinely useful on its own: run it across the cluster and read  as a free, continuously updated right-sizing report, without letting it touch anything. Historically  mode had to evict Pods to change resources, which made it disruptive; in-place Pod resize graduated to stable in Kubernetes 1.35, and pod-level in-place resize reached beta in 1.36, so on current clusters resource changes can increasingly be applied without a restart. Check what your cluster version and your VPA release actually support before relying on it.

Cluster Autoscaler and Karpenter

The HPA adds Pods. Something has to add nodes when those Pods do not fit.

| | Cluster Autoscaler | Karpenter ||---|---|---|| Model | scales pre-defined node groups / ASGs up and down | provisions individual nodes chosen to fit the pending Pods || Instance choice | fixed per node group | picks instance type, size, zone and capacity type per batch of Pods || Speed | minutes (ASG-mediated) | typically faster; talks to the cloud API directly || Consolidation | scale-down of under-used nodes | active consolidation and bin-packing; replaces nodes with cheaper ones || Availability | all major clouds | AWS-native origin, now with other providers |

Both react to unschedulable Pods, which means both depend entirely on your requests being accurate. Requests that are too high make the autoscaler buy capacity nobody uses; requests that are too low let it under-provision and then evict. Note also that Karpenter-style consolidation deliberately churns nodes, so PodDisruptionBudgets and correct graceful shutdown (Chapter 5) stop being optional.

Right-sizing methodology

A repeatable procedure, in order:

1. Instrument first. Without  and a metrics store you are guessing. Collect at least two weeks, covering a weekly cycle and any peak event.2. Set memory request at P95 of observed working set; set the memory limit at the observed peak plus 25-50 %. Memory is incompressible, so the limit is a safety fuse, not a target.3. Set CPU request at P90 of observed usage. This is the scheduling reservation and the HPA's denominator, so it should reflect typical, not peak.4. Decide the CPU limit by policy (Chapter 3), then verify with the throttling ratio.5. Check the QoS class you ended up with —  — and confirm it matches the workload's importance.6. Compare requests against actual usage cluster-wide. The ratio of total requests to total usage is your headline waste number, and it is usually far worse than anyone expects.7. Re-measure after every significant release. Right-sizing is not a one-off project.

Read those two together: a memory ratio consistently near 1.0 predicts OOMKills; a CPU ratio consistently near 0.05 is money on fire.

Configure an HPA with  on both directions and drive it with a spiky load. Record the replica count over 15 minutes. Then apply asymmetric behaviour (fast up, 10-minute down window) and compare replica churn and p99 latency.

Exercise 7 — Right-size a real workload

Pick one Deployment. Pull 7 days of CPU and memory usage. Compute P90 CPU and P95 memory, set requests accordingly, set the memory limit at peak + 30 %, and measure the throttling ratio before and after. Report the change in requested capacity and in p99 latency.
