They are object-model bugs: a callback that lost its , a class field that silently shadowed a base-class setter, a "deep copy" that was one level deep, a  that broke private fields, a cache that kept every request alive. Each of these follows directly from rules in the specification that are short, precise and rarely taught in full. Learning Outcome

- Explain scopes as environment records, and state exactly when the temporal dead zone (TDZ) applies.- Recognise the closure-retention leak caused by shared scope contexts.- Apply the four  rules in order of precedence, plus arrow functions and class fields.- Draw the prototype chain for a class hierarchy, including the constructor-side chain.- Use private ,  brand checks, static blocks and accessors, and know how class fields differ from assignments.- Read and write property descriptors; choose between ,  and .- Implement the iterator protocol, use generators for lazy pipelines, and hook into well-known symbols.- Build a  that forwards correctly with , and respect its invariants.- Choose between ,  and , and between , spread and JSON for copies.

Chapter 1 — Scope, closures and the TDZ

Terminology

- Environment record — the specification's name for a scope: a map from names to bindings. Function calls, blocks, modules and the global scope each create one. Each record points to its outer record; name lookup walks that chain.- Binding — a named slot.  and function declarations create bindings that are initialised to  (or the function) when the scope is entered. ,  and  create bindings that stay uninitialised until their declaration runs.- TDZ (temporal dead zone) — the time between entering a scope and executing a // declaration. Any access to the binding in that window throws .- Closure — a function object plus a reference to the environment where it was created.

The TDZ, precisely

The TDZ is temporal, not textual: what matters is when the access runs, not where it is written.

Other TDZ cases that bite in real code: a  used before its declaration in the same module (classes are not hoisted like functions), and circular ES module imports, where a module reads an imported  before the exporting module has evaluated it — the error message names the binding, not the cycle.

Closures capture bindings, not values

The shared-context leak

V8 (like other engines) does not give each closure its own copy of the variables it uses. Variables captured by any closure in a scope go into one context object for that scope, and every closure created in that scope keeps the whole context alive:

Keeping 20 of the returned closures alive retained about 164 MB of heap in Node 22; the same code without  retained about 4 MB. Real versions of this leak involve an event handler or a timer callback created in the same function as a closure that touched a large response or DOM tree.

> Trick: when a long-lived callback is created in a function that also handles big data, move the callback's creation into a small separate function that receives only what it needs. The new scope has its own context, and the big object is no longer reachable.

| Rule | Call shape |  is ||---|---|---|| 1.  |  | The newly created object (a bound function's bound  is ignored) || 2. Explicit | , ,  |  || 3. Implicit (method) | ,  |  — the object before the dot, at the moment of the call || 4. Default |  |  in strict code (modules and classes are always strict);  in sloppy scripts || Arrow functions | any | The  of the enclosing scope; / cannot change it |

Where it goes wrong in practice:

- Passing methods as callbacks — , , . For DOM listeners  becomes the element; for the others, .- Destructuring methods —  works only because  methods happen not to rely on  in modern engines;  does not.

Fixes, with their trade-offs:

> Watch out: arrow-function class fields are convenient, but they live on each instance, not on the prototype. They cannot be overridden with  in a subclass, do not appear on the prototype for mocking in tests, and cost one closure per instance — noticeable for classes instantiated thousands of times.

Chapter 3 — The prototype chain

Every object has an internal slot  — another object or . Reading a property walks the chain until it finds an own property with that key; writing does something subtler.

What  builds

There are two chains:

- Instance side:  →  (has ) →  (has ) →  → .- Constructor side:  →  →  →  → . This is why  works: static methods are inherited because .

Facts that follow from the spec and matter in code review:

- Class methods are non-enumerable, so they never show up in ,  or spread.- Class bodies are strict mode, and calling a class without  throws .-  is resolved through the method's home object (the object it was defined on), not through , so it keeps working when the method is borrowed.-  checks whether  appears on the chain (unless  defines ). It fails across realms — an array from an iframe is not  in the parent. Prefer , or brand checks (Chapter 4).

Writing is not symmetric with reading

does not simply create an own property. If  is found on the prototype chain:

| Found on the chain as | Result of  ||---|---|| Nothing | Creates an own data property on  || A writable data property | Creates an own property on  (shadowing) || A non-writable data property | Assignment fails:  in strict mode, silently ignored in sloppy mode. No own property is created || An accessor (setter) | Calls the inherited setter with ; no own property is created |

This asymmetry between assignment (, follows the chain) and definition (, does not) is the key to class fields in the next chapter.

> Common pitfall: using a plain object as a dictionary of user-supplied keys.  hits the inherited  accessor and changes the object's prototype instead of storing a key — the root of many prototype-pollution vulnerabilities. Use , or  for a chain-free object.

Chapter 4 — Classes in depth

Fields are defined, not assigned

Class fields use define semantics (like ), not assignment:

The own property  now shadows the base-class accessor. Code migrated from constructor assignments (, which would call the setter) to fields changes behaviour. TypeScript follows the standard when  is  (the default for targets ES2022 and later); older configurations emitted assignments, so upgrading the target can change behaviour for exactly this pattern.

Private members and brand checks

Private names are lexically scoped to the class body and are not properties at all: they are not reachable by , ,  traps or . Accessing  on an object that lacks it throws  — which makes  a reliable, realm-independent brand check, unlike .

> Watch out: private fields and  do not mix. A method called through a proxy has , and the proxy object has no private fields:>>>> Any library that wraps your objects in proxies (Vue's  is the best-known example) hits exactly this. Either keep such classes out of proxied state, or have their methods work on the raw target.

| | Add properties | Delete | Reconfigure | Change values ||---|---|---|---|---||  | No | Yes | Yes | Yes ||  | No | No | No | Yes ||  | No | No | No | No (data properties) |

Chapter 6 — Symbols, iterators and generators

Symbols

A  is a unique, non-string property key. ;  returns a shared, registry-wide symbol (the same across realms). Symbol-keyed properties are skipped by ,  and , but are copied by spread and , and listed by . They are hidden from casual enumeration, not private.

Well-known symbols are the engine's hooks into your objects:

| Symbol | Hook | Used by ||---|---|---||  | Return an iterator | , spread, destructuring, ,  ||  | Return an async iterator |  ||  | Convert to a primitive, given a hint (, , ) | , template literals,  ||  | Custom  |  ||  | Tag for  |  in logs and type checks ||  | Constructor for derived objects | / on  subclasses,  on  subclasses || ,  | Cleanup hook |  /  declarations (explicit resource management; shipped in Chrome 134 and Node 24, check other engines; Post 03) |

The iteration protocols

An iterable has a  method returning an iterator: an object with  returning , and optionally .  calls  when the loop exits early (, , ) — this is how iterators release resources.

Generators

A generator function returns an object that is both iterable and an iterator, with the protocol implemented for you. It runs lazily, one  at a time, and its  blocks run when a consumer stops early:

Since ES2025, iterator helpers give every built-in iterator lazy , , , , , , , , ,  and , and  wraps any iterable:

Nothing is computed until  pulls values, so an infinite source is fine. Unlike array methods, no intermediate arrays are allocated.

> Trick:  delegates to another iterable and forwards  and  to it, so composed generators still clean up correctly. Hand-written iterator wrappers usually forget to forward  and leak the inner resource.

Chapter 7 —  and

A  wraps a target and intercepts the object's internal methods through traps: , , , , , , , , , , , and for functions  and .  has a method with the same name and signature for every trap, performing the default behaviour.

Forward with , and pass the receiver

The  argument is the object the operation started on. Forwarding it is what makes getters, setters and inheritance (a proxy used as a prototype) behave correctly.

Invariants

A proxy may lie, but not about facts the target has made permanent. The engine checks each trap's result against the target and throws  on a violation. The main ones:

-  must return the actual value for a non-writable, non-configurable own data property of the target.-  and  cannot hide a non-configurable property, or any property of a non-extensible target.-  must include every non-configurable own key; for a non-extensible target it must list exactly the target's keys.-  must match the target;  must match for a non-extensible target.

This is why you cannot make a "read-only view" of a frozen object that rewrites values, and why reactive systems proxy mutable targets.

Costs and limits

- Identity: . Sets, maps and  checks see two different objects; libraries keep a  from target to proxy to return the same proxy each time.- Internal slots: built-ins keep data in internal slots that a proxy does not have.  throws ( called on an incompatible receiver); the same applies to , , typed arrays and private fields.- Speed: every trapped operation is a function call, and proxied access is much slower than a plain property load in optimised code (Post 00's inline caches cannot see through a trap). Keep proxies at boundaries — validation, reactivity, API clients — not in hot inner loops.-  returns ; after , every operation on the proxy throws. Useful for handing out capabilities that can be withdrawn.

Chapter 8 — Weak references and copying

Weak collections

| | Keys / target | Enumerable | Typical use ||---|---|---|---||  | Objects and non-registered symbols | No | Per-object metadata and caches that die with the object ||  | Same | No | Marking objects ("already visited", "already wrapped") ||  | One object | — | A cache entry that may disappear;  returns the object or  ||  | Callback after an object is collected | — | Releasing an external resource as a backstop |

A  entry keeps its value alive only while the key is alive — the right structure for "extra data about objects I do not own" (DOM nodes, request objects, library instances):

and  are deliberately weak guarantees. The specification allows a finalization callback to run late or never (for example, when the page closes). Within one synchronous job,  keeps returning the same object (the engine keeps it alive until the job ends), so check once and use the result. Never put essential cleanup — closing a file, releasing a lock — in a finalizer; use  or , and treat the registry as a leak detector or a last-resort backstop.

Copying:  vs spread vs JSON

| | Spread /  |  |  ||---|---|---|---|| Depth | Shallow | Deep | Deep ||  | Same object shared | Becomes a string |  || ,  | Same object shared | Becomes  | ,  ||  values | Kept | Key dropped | Kept || ,  | Kept | Become  | Kept ||  | Kept | Throws | Kept || Circular references | Shared (shallow) | Throws | Preserved || Functions | Copied by reference | Dropped |  || Getters | Invoked; value copied | Invoked; value copied | Invoked; value copied || Prototype / class | Lost (plain object) | Lost | Lost || Symbol keys | Copied | Dropped | Dropped || Private  | Not copied | Not copied | Not copied |

(Cells checked in Node 22.) The practical rules:

- Spread for one level of immutable update (), which is what React and Redux reducers need.-  for a real deep copy of data — including , , , typed arrays, cycles.- Neither preserves behaviour: class instances come back as plain objects. To copy an instance, give the class its own  method.- JSON round-trips are for serialisation, not copying.

References

- ECMAScript specification — Environment Records · Ordinary object internal methods · Proxy object internal methods and invariants- MDN —- MDN — Inheritance and the prototype chain- MDN — Public class fields · Private properties · Static initialization blocks- MDN —- MDN — Iteration protocols · Iterator helpers ()- MDN —  ·- MDN —  ·- MDN —- TypeScript —
15:T5257,"JavaScript is single-threaded" is true of your code on one thread and false of everything around it. The browser runs networking, parsing, compositing and garbage collection on other threads; Node runs file-system work on a thread pool; and both let you start real parallel threads of JavaScript with workers. What is single-threaded is each agent's event loop: one call stack, fed by queues of tasks and microtasks, in an order that is precisely specified — by the HTML standard in browsers and by libuv plus Node's own rules on the server. This post makes that order exact: tasks vs microtasks, where rendering and  fit, the ordering puzzles that come out of it, Node's phases with  and , and then true parallelism with workers, structured clone, transferables,  and . It ends with the production view: long tasks, INP and yielding with .

Chapter 1 — The model

Terminology

- Agent — a call stack, a set of queues and one event loop. A window (with its same-origin iframes), each worker, and each Node  worker are separate agents.- Task (often "macrotask") — a unit of work queued by the host: a timer firing, an input event, a network response, a . Browsers keep several task queues (by task source) and may choose which queue to serve next — this is how input can be prioritised over timers.- Microtask — a smaller unit queued by promise reactions, , and . There is one microtask queue per agent.- Microtask checkpoint — the step that runs microtasks until the queue is empty, including microtasks queued by microtasks.- Rendering opportunity — a moment when the browser can present a new frame, typically aligned with the display refresh (about every 16.7 ms at 60 Hz). Browsers may skip frames or throttle hidden pages.

One turn of the browser loop

1. Pick the oldest task from a task queue the browser chooses, and run it to completion. The call stack empties.2. Perform a microtask checkpoint: run microtasks until none remain.3. If this is a rendering opportunity and there is something to render, update the rendering (Chapter 3).4. Repeat.

Two rules produce nearly every "surprising" order:

- Microtasks run whenever the JavaScript stack becomes empty, not only between tasks. The HTML spec's "clean up after running script" step performs a microtask checkpoint whenever the JavaScript execution context stack becomes empty — after a task, but also after each event listener the browser invokes and after each  callback.- The checkpoint drains the queue completely. A microtask that queues another microtask keeps the loop in step 2. An infinite chain of microtasks freezes the page as surely as  — rendering and input never get a turn.

> Watch out:  is not "run next". HTML clamps the delay to at least 4 ms once timers are nested more than 5 levels deep, browsers throttle timers in background tabs heavily, and a timer is only a task — everything already queued, and the rendering step, can run first.

Chapter 2 — Ordering puzzles

Work each one out before reading the answer. All outputs below were checked in Node 22 (the same rules apply in browsers for these cases).

Puzzle 1 — the basics

Output: .

- , ,  are synchronous. The body of an  function runs synchronously up to its first .- Three microtasks are queued in order: the  callback (3), the  callback (4), the continuation after  (6).- The timer is a task, so  comes after the microtask checkpoint.

Puzzle 2 — async functions calling async functions

Output: , , , , , , , .

The promise executor () runs synchronously.  returns an already-fulfilled native promise; since ES2019,  on a native promise takes a single microtask hop, so  is queued before . (Older engines needed three hops and printed  first — a well-known behaviour change, which is why old blog posts disagree.)

Puzzle 3 — returning a promise from

Output: .

Returning a thenable from a  callback does not resolve the outer promise directly. The spec queues a , which calls , which queues another job when  is already fulfilled. Two extra hops, so  lands after .

| Microtask queue after each step | Queue contents ||---|---|| start | ,  || run  (returns a promise) | , thenable job || run  | thenable job,  || run thenable job () | , resolve outer || run  | resolve outer,  || resolve outer (queues ) | ,  || run  | ,  |

> Trick: in an  function,  inside  is needed for the  to see a rejection, but plain  costs the same extra hops as Puzzle 3. The difference in hops almost never matters; the difference in error handling often does. Choose by semantics.

Puzzle 4 — user click vs

- Real user click: , , , . The browser calls each listener from the event loop, the stack empties after each, and a microtask checkpoint runs in between.-  from script: , , , . The listeners run inside your script's call stack, which is not empty until  returns and your script finishes.

This is why tests that dispatch synthetic events can pass while the real UI behaves differently — for example when a listener relies on a microtask (such as a framework's batched update) having run before the next listener.

Chapter 3 — Rendering and

When the loop decides to render, the HTML standard's update the rendering step runs, per document, in this order (abridged):

1. Run the resize steps (fire ), then the scroll steps (fire ).2. Evaluate media queries; update animations and send animation events; fullscreen steps.3. Run the animation frame callbacks — every  callback registered before this frame began.4. Recalculate styles and update layout, then deliver  callbacks; if they changed layout, recalculate and deliver again (to a depth limit, after which the browser reports a "ResizeObserver loop" error).5. Run the  update steps.6. Paint — "update the rendering or user interface".

Consequences for engineers:

- rAF is the place for visual writes. Code in a rAF callback runs just before style and layout, so the frame shows its result. A  animation drifts against the display's refresh and may run twice or zero times per frame.- A rAF callback that calls rAF schedules for the next frame, not the current one. That is what makes rAF loops work.- Microtasks run between rAF callbacks (the stack empties after each one), so a promise resolved in one rAF callback settles before the next callback runs.- Layout thrashing: reading layout (, ) after writing styles forces the browser to do style and layout synchronously, in the middle of your task. Doing it in a loop — write, read, write, read — can force a layout per iteration. Batch all reads first, then all writes.

> Common pitfall: using rAF in a background tab to keep "real-time" work going. Browsers stop or heavily throttle rAF in hidden documents (the HTML spec removes hidden documents from rendering). Anything that must keep running — a WebSocket heartbeat, a timer for session expiry — belongs in a timer or a worker, not rAF.

Chapter 4 — The Node.js event loop

Node's loop is libuv's loop plus two queues Node adds on top.

| Phase | Runs ||---|---|| timers | Expired  /  callbacks || pending callbacks | Some system I/O callbacks deferred from the previous iteration (e.g. certain TCP errors) || idle, prepare | Internal only || poll | Retrieves new I/O events and runs their callbacks; the loop blocks here when there is nothing else to do || check |  callbacks || close callbacks |  events such as  |

The Node documentation notes one change: starting with libuv 1.45.0 (Node 20), timers are run after the poll phase in each iteration; earlier versions ran them before polling.

On top of the phases:

-  queue — drained after the current operation completes, before the loop moves on, regardless of phase.- Microtask queue (promises, ) — drained right after the nextTick queue. Every time Node drains the nextTick queue, it drains the microtask queue immediately after.- Since Node 11, both queues are drained between every individual callback (after each timer, each immediate), matching browser behaviour. Older Node drained them only between phases.

vs

From the main module the order is not guaranteed — it depends on how quickly the loop starts relative to the 1 ms minimum timer. Inside an I/O callback it is deterministic: the loop is in the poll phase, so the check phase () always comes before the next timers phase.

vs promises: CommonJS vs ES modules

| File type | Output | Why ||---|---|---||  (CommonJS) | , ,  | The main script is an ordinary operation; the nextTick queue drains first, then microtasks ||  (ES module) | , ,  | ES module evaluation already runs as part of the microtask queue, so microtasks queued there run before Node gets back to the nextTick queue |

This is the example from the Node documentation, and it is the reason code that relied on  ordering changes behaviour after a CommonJS-to-ESM migration.

> Trick: Node's own advice is to prefer  over  for deferring work in userland, and  over  for "after I/O". Reach for  only when you need its specific guarantee — for example, emitting an event after the caller has had a chance to attach listeners, in a constructor.

> Watch out: a recursive  (or a recursive microtask) starves I/O. The poll phase never runs, so sockets are not read and timers never fire. A recursive  does not starve I/O: each round waits for the next check phase.

What runs off the main thread in Node

The event loop is single-threaded, but libuv has a thread pool (default 4 threads, ) for work without a good asynchronous OS API: most  operations, , and CPU-heavy  and  calls. Network sockets do not use the pool; they use the OS's readiness APIs (epoll, kqueue, IOCP). A burst of  calls or  calls can therefore queue behind four busy threads — a classic hidden bottleneck.

Chapter 5 — Parallelism: workers

When work is CPU-bound — parsing a large file, image processing, compression, heavy validation — no scheduling trick helps; it must run on another thread.

| | Web Worker (browser) |  (Node) ||---|---|---|| Create |  |  || Communicate |  / ,  |  / , ,  || DOM access | None | Not applicable || Shared memory | , only when cross-origin isolated | , always || Memory limits | Browser-managed |  option (e.g. ) |

Each worker is a separate agent: its own isolate, its own heap, its own event loop. Starting one costs milliseconds and several megabytes, so create a pool once and reuse it rather than spawning a worker per request.

Structured clone and transferables

copies its argument with the structured clone algorithm (the same one behind ):

| Copied | Not copied (throws  or is lost) ||---|---|| Primitives except symbols;  | Functions, symbols →  || Plain objects, arrays, , , ,  | DOM nodes →  || , typed arrays, , ,  | Prototypes: a class instance arrives as a plain object || Error objects (, , …) | Private , getters/setters (only the current value is copied) || Circular references (preserved) | Property descriptors (everything becomes writable, enumerable) |

Copying a 100 MB buffer costs time and doubles memory. Transferring moves ownership instead: the sender's  becomes detached ( 0) and the receiver gets the same memory with no copy. Transferable types include , , , , and the stream types ,  and .

> Trick:  lets a worker own a canvas and render without touching the main thread: . Charting and map libraries use this to keep the main thread free for input.

Chapter 6 — Shared memory and

Message passing shares nothing, which is why it is safe.  shares memory: both threads see the same bytes, with all the hazards of real threads.

Why browsers require cross-origin isolation

Shared memory plus a busy-loop counter gives a high-resolution timer, which is enough to mount Spectre-style side-channel attacks that read memory across origins. Browsers therefore expose  only to cross-origin isolated documents, which requires two response headers on the top-level document:

Check  at run time.  blocks every cross-origin subresource that does not opt in with CORS or  — third-party scripts, images and iframes break — so adopting it is a project, not a header change. Without isolation,  of a  throws. Node has no such restriction.

Data races and

operations are indivisible and sequentially consistent:

| Operation | Use ||---|---||  /  | Read / write with ordering guarantees || , , , , ,  | Read-modify-write, returns the old value ||  | The building block for locks and lock-free structures ||  | Sleep while . Throws on a browser main thread; fine in workers and Node ||  | Non-blocking wait that returns  (a promise when  is true); usable on the main thread; Baseline since November 2025 ||  | Wake waiters |

> Watch out:  gives you raw bytes, not objects. You cannot share a JavaScript object graph between threads; you design a binary layout (a ring buffer, a struct of typed-array fields) and serialise into it. That is the right tool for audio, video, physics or WASM threads — and the wrong tool for sharing application state, where message passing is simpler and safer.

Chapter 7 — Long tasks, INP and yielding

Terminology

- Long task — any task that occupies the main thread for more than 50 ms. Input that arrives during it waits.- INP (Interaction to Next Paint) — the Core Web Vital for responsiveness: roughly the worst latency from a user interaction to the next frame, across the page's life. Good is 200 ms or less; poor is above 500 ms.- Yielding — ending the current task deliberately so the browser can handle input and render, then continuing in a new task.

Yielding the right way

Splitting work with  works, but the continuation goes to the back of the task queue, behind every other queued task (and third-party scripts).  returns a promise whose continuation is queued with priority above other tasks of the same priority, so your work resumes promptly after the browser has handled input and rendering:

shipped in Chrome 129 and Firefox 142; at the time of writing it is not Baseline, so always feature-detect.  is the companion API for scheduling new work with a priority. Microtasks are not a way to yield:  resumes before the browser can render or handle input.

Finding the long tasks

- DevTools Performance panel: long tasks are flagged with a red corner; the flame chart shows which function owns the time.- Long Animation Frames API (LoAF):  reports frames that took longer than 50 ms, with script attribution (source URL, function, invoker). It is the field-data tool for explaining a bad INP.- Node:  gives a histogram of event-loop delay; a p99 in the hundreds of milliseconds means a synchronous hot spot (JSON parsing a huge body, a sync crypto call, a regex with catastrophic backtracking).

> Common pitfall: "we moved it into a promise, so it's async now". Wrapping CPU-bound work in  or an  function changes nothing — the executor and the code before the first  run synchronously, and the rest runs in microtasks on the same thread. Only yielding (new tasks) or a worker (new thread) keeps the page responsive.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Each microtask queues another, and the checkpoint only ends when the queue is empty, so the loop never reaches the rendering step or the next task. Replace  with  (or ): each iteration becomes a separate task, and rendering and input can happen between them.

. After the I/O callback returns, Node drains the nextTick queue, then microtasks. The loop is in the poll phase, so the check phase () comes before the next timers phase.

Exercise 4 — The module-format bug

A library emits  from its constructor with . The application attaches the listener one microtask later:

In the ESM application this logs . The same code in the CommonJS test suite never does. Explain, and fix the library.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

In a  file the top-level code already runs inside the microtask queue, so the promise callback (which attaches the listener) runs before Node returns to the nextTick queue, and the event is caught. In a  file the nextTick queue drains first:  is emitted before anyone listens, and it is lost (both outputs checked in Node 22). The code only ever worked by accident of queue order. Fix the library so late listeners cannot miss the state: expose it ( as a promise that stays resolved, or an  flag the caller checks first), instead of a one-shot event fired on a timing guess.

</details>

(a) Main thread; the work is small — debounce input and yield if it grows. (b) Worker: transfer the  from  in, stream results back in chunks. Each chunk stays under about 50 ms, so no long task blocks input for longer than that; the first yield lets the spinner render. For much larger lists, virtualise the list instead of rendering every item.

</details>

Chapter 1 — CloudWatch metrics

Every AWS service publishes metrics to CloudWatch automatically:

- EC2 — CPU, network in/out, status checks.- ALB — request count, target response time, 5XX count, healthy host count.- Lambda — invocations, errors, throttles, duration, concurrent executions.- RDS — CPU, free memory, IOPS, replica lag.- DynamoDB — consumed read/write capacity, throttles.

Metrics are stored per namespace (e.g., ) with dimensions (e.g., ). Each is a time series of values.

Custom metrics

For Lambdas, Embedded Metric Format (EMF) is cheaper — write a specific JSON line to logs, CloudWatch extracts it as a metric automatically.

Chapter 2 — CloudWatch Logs

Every Lambda, ECS task, EC2 (with CloudWatch Agent), API Gateway, RDS (optional), and ALB (optional) writes logs to log groups.

- Group:  or .- Stream: one per execution / container / day.- Each log event has a timestamp + message.

Retention

The default retention is forever — costly over time. Set retention on every log group: 7, 14, 30, or 90 days are typical.

Querying

CloudWatch Logs Insights is a powerful query language:

> Trick: the most-overlooked CloudWatch cost is log ingest. A chatty Lambda producing 5 MB/min over a month = USD 1.30/GB × 200 GB = USD 260. Demote  to  for prod, sample noisy lines, and set short retention.

Chapter 3 — Alarms

A CloudWatch alarm watches a metric and notifies (usually via SNS) when a threshold is breached.

Minimum production alarms:

| Resource | Alarm ||----------|-------|| Every Lambda |  || Every Lambda |  || Every ALB |  || Every ALB |  || Every RDS |  || Every RDS |  || Every RDS |  || Every DLQ |  || Every NAT gateway |  || Account |  (in us-east-1) |

The SNS topic typically subscribes email + a Slack/Teams webhook + PagerDuty.

Chapter 4 — Dashboards

CloudWatch dashboards combine metrics + logs widgets into a single page. Reasonable starter set:

- Service KPI tile: request rate, p50 / p95 latency, 5xx count.- Resource utilisation row: CPU, memory, errors per service.- Cost tile: estimated charges + month-over-month.- Dependency row: DB CPU, queue depth, cache hit rate.

For multi-account visibility, CloudWatch cross-account observability lets one monitoring account aggregate metrics + logs from many workload accounts.

Alternatives

Datadog, Grafana / Prometheus, New Relic, Honeycomb — all integrate with CloudWatch. Pick whichever your team uses if it's already paid for. Pure-AWS dashboards are fine to start.

Chapter 5 — X-Ray (distributed tracing)

X-Ray traces a request as it hops between services (API Gateway → Lambda → DynamoDB → SNS). For each segment: start time, end time, latency, errors. Find bottlenecks in seconds.

Enable per service:- Lambda: tick "Active tracing" or .- API Gateway: enable in stage settings.- ECS: add the X-Ray daemon container.

In the SDK, instrument client calls:

X-Ray is being superseded by the newer AWS Distro for OpenTelemetry (ADOT) for cross-cloud + OSS compatibility. For pure AWS, X-Ray is still the simplest start.

Chapter 6 — CloudTrail (audit log)

CloudTrail records every API call in your account: who, when, what, from where.

By default, a free 90-day event history is available in the console. For longer retention and analytical use, configure a trail that writes to S3 + optionally CloudWatch Logs.

What CloudTrail tells you

- Who created / deleted resources.- Who assumed a role.- Failed authentication attempts.- Console logins (with MFA status).- Source IPs of API calls.

Couple with Athena to query CloudTrail logs in S3 cheaply.

Chapter 7 — Systems Manager (SSM)

SSM is a Swiss-army-knife. Four parts everyone uses:

| Part | What ||------|------|| Parameter Store | hierarchical key-value config; free for standard parameters || Secrets Manager (technically separate but adjacent) | rotating secrets, KMS-encrypted || Session Manager | shell on EC2 without SSH (Post 02) || Patch Manager | scheduled OS patching of EC2 / on-prem || Run Command | execute scripts across fleets || State Manager | enforce config (cron-like maintenance) || Inventory | what's installed where |

Parameter Store quick start

Apps fetch with the SDK at startup. Or use Lambda Extensions / EC2 user-data to pre-pull.

Chapter 8 — Secrets Manager

Specialised for credentials with automatic rotation. AWS rotates RDS / DocumentDB / Redshift passwords without app-code changes when wired correctly.

| Trait | Parameter Store SecureString | Secrets Manager ||-------|-------------------------------|------------------|| Cost | free | USD 0.40 / secret / month || Rotation | manual | built-in (RDS, custom Lambda) || Cross-account | manual | native sharing || Versioning | yes | yes |

For non-rotating config, Parameter Store SecureString is cheaper. For real credentials with rotation, use Secrets Manager.

> Trick: never store secrets in environment variables visible in the Lambda console / CloudFormation. Fetch from Secrets Manager at startup and cache in memory.

Chapter 9 — KMS (encryption keys)

KMS (Key Management Service) manages cryptographic keys for AWS services + your own code. Two key kinds:

| Kind | Use ||------|-----|| AWS-managed | each service has a default (, , etc.); free || Customer-managed (CMK) | you control rotation, key policy, deletion; USD 1 / month / key |

For most workloads, the default AWS-managed key is enough. Move to CMKs when:- Compliance demands it (HIPAA, FedRAMP).- You want to deny access to specific principals via key policy.- You want to enforce annual rotation.

Then use the key when creating an S3 bucket, RDS instance, EBS volume, etc., via the  flag.

Chapter 10 — WAF and Shield

| Service | Protects | Cost ||---------|----------|------|| AWS WAF | ALB / API Gateway / CloudFront — application-layer (L7) | per-WebACL + per-rule + per-million requests || AWS Shield Standard | always-on DDoS protection for all AWS resources | free || AWS Shield Advanced | enhanced DDoS + 24/7 DRT support + cost protection | USD 3,000 / month / org |

Minimum WAF setup

A WebACL with these managed rule groups attached:-  — OWASP top 10 staples.-  — known exploit payloads.-  — known bad IPs.-  (paid) — bot detection.- A per-IP rate-based rule (e.g., block IP at 2000 req / 5 min).

Cost: USD 5 / WebACL + USD 1 / rule + USD 0.60 / million requests + USD 10 / rule for Bot Control. Affordable for most production sites.

Chapter 11 — GuardDuty and Security Hub

| Service | What ||---------|------|| GuardDuty | continuous threat detection (DNS exfil, crypto mining, unusual API patterns, leaked credentials) || Security Hub | aggregates findings from GuardDuty + Inspector + Macie + 3rd parties; scores against standards (CIS, PCI) || Config | tracks resource config + compliance against rules || Inspector | EC2 / Lambda / ECR vulnerability scanning |

A modern baseline enables GuardDuty + Security Hub + Config in every region of every account, ideally via an AWS Organizations delegated-admin pattern. Cost is modest relative to a single breach.

Chapter 12 — The production observability + security checklist

For each new workload:

Observability- [ ] Every Lambda / service writes structured logs with correlation IDs.- [ ] Every log group has retention ≤ 90 days.- [ ] Every service has dashboards for KPI + utilisation.- [ ] Every critical metric has an alarm with paging SNS subscribers.- [ ] X-Ray (or OpenTelemetry) is on across the request path.- [ ] Cost alarms exist at account level.

Security- [ ] Root user MFA enabled; root credentials in a vault.- [ ] CloudTrail multi-region trail to encrypted S3, log validation on.- [ ] GuardDuty enabled in all regions.- [ ] Security Hub aggregating findings.- [ ] No long-lived IAM access keys for code; everything uses roles.- [ ] Secrets in Secrets Manager, not in env vars or code.- [ ] S3 buckets: Block Public Access on, encryption on.- [ ] WAF in front of every internet-facing ALB / API Gateway.- [ ] Critical resources protected with deletion-protection / S3 Object Lock for backups.- [ ] Backup retention reviewed (RDS, EBS snapshots, DynamoDB PITR, S3 versioning).

Practice Exercises

Exercise 1 — Lambda error alarm

For the Lambda from Post 05, create an alarm on . Send notifications to an SNS topic. Subscribe your email. Force an error; confirm the alarm + email fire.

Exercise 2 — Log Insights query

Write a CloudWatch Logs Insights query that finds the 10 slowest invocations of a Lambda over the last hour.

Exercise 3 — Parameter Store + Secrets Manager

Move all the configuration (DB host, API keys, secrets) for one of your previous-post Lambdas out of environment variables: non-secret → Parameter Store, secrets → Secrets Manager. The function fetches at cold start, caches in module-level vars.

Exercise 4 — CloudTrail audit

In the CloudTrail event history console, find the API call that created your first VPC. Note the user, source IP, and timestamp.

Exercise 5 — WAF baseline

Attach a WebACL to the ALB from Post 03 with  and a per-IP rate rule of 100 req / 5 min. Test with  or  that the rate rule actually blocks.

Exercise 6 — Enable GuardDuty

Enable GuardDuty in one region. Create a deliberate "suspicious" trigger (e.g., resolve a known-bad domain from an EC2). Wait for the finding to appear.

Cheat-sheet

| What | How ||------|-----|| Metric publish |  or EMF JSON line || Log group retention |  || Alarm |  → SNS topic → email/Slack/PagerDuty || Dashboard | console widgets, or JSON via  || Distributed tracing | X-Ray / OpenTelemetry || Audit log | CloudTrail multi-region trail to S3 || Config / non-secret | SSM Parameter Store || Secret with rotation | Secrets Manager || Customer-managed key | KMS CMK with annual rotation || L7 firewall | WAF managed rule groups + rate rule || DDoS basic | Shield Standard (free, always on) || Threat detection | GuardDuty + Security Hub || Vulnerability scan | Inspector || Resource config | Config rules + conformance packs || Log noise = cost | demote , sample, short retention |

References

- CloudWatch User Guide- CloudTrail User Guide- Systems Manager User Guide- KMS Developer Guide- WAF Developer Guide- GuardDuty User Guide- AWS Whitepaper — AWS Well-Architected Security Pillar.

> Note: this post is in progress. Planned additions: CloudWatch synthetics, AWS Config conformance pack examples, IAM Access Analyzer walkthrough, OpenTelemetry collector deployment.
17:T22e5,Once you have many Lambdas + events (Post 05), the next problem is coordination: "run A, then B in parallel with C, then if A succeeded do D, retry B up to 3 times, and if anything fails roll back". Wiring this with raw Lambdas and SQS is possible but quickly turns into spaghetti. AWS Step Functions provides a managed state-machine service exactly for this purpose. This post covers the basics of Step Functions, the two workflow types (Standard vs Express), the common patterns (sequence, parallel, choice, map, wait, retry, catch), the Saga pattern for distributed transactions, and when to reach for Step Functions vs simpler alternatives.

> Series map: 00 Foundations → 01 Network First → 02 Compute & Storage → 03 Scale & Survive → 04 Managed Data → 05 Serverless & Events → 06 Workflow Orchestration (this post) → 07 Observability & Security → 08 IaC.

Learning Outcome

- Write a state machine in Amazon States Language (ASL).- Pick between Standard and Express workflows.- Use the core state types: , , , , , , , .- Configure retries, catches, and timeouts.- Implement a Saga (compensating-action rollback) for distributed transactions.- Decide between Step Functions, EventBridge Pipes, and SQS-chained Lambdas.

Chapter 1 — Why state machines

A workflow is a graph of steps with branches, parallelism, error handling, and (sometimes) human approval. Hand-rolled, this turns into a tangle of Lambdas invoking other Lambdas, with retry logic copied in each, error-handling baked into each step, and no central view of "where is this execution right now?".

Step Functions externalises the orchestration:

- The state machine definition is a JSON document — version-controlled, auditable.- Step Functions runs each execution: tracks position, retries, errors, history.- The console shows a visual diagram of every execution — which step succeeded, which failed, what data flowed where.- Each step is small and stateless (your Lambdas or AWS service calls); orchestration logic lives in one place.

Chapter 2 — Workflow types

| Type | Duration | Pricing | Execution history | Use ||------|----------|---------|-------------------|------|| Standard | up to 1 year | per state transition | full history, queryable | long-running, audit-required, occasional || Express | up to 5 min | per execution time | CloudWatch Logs only | high-throughput, short |

Standard suits business workflows (order fulfilment, ETL, approvals). Express suits per-request orchestration (an API call that needs to fan out, gather, return).

Chapter 3 — Amazon States Language (ASL)

A state machine is a JSON document with  + :

Run:

Every state has a . The major ones follow.

Chapter 4 — Core state types

Task

Invokes an AWS service action. The most common is a Lambda, but Step Functions can call > 200 AWS service actions directly without a Lambda:

The  suffix tells the engine "use the value from the execution state at this JSONPath". This service integration avoids a Lambda hop entirely — faster + cheaper + simpler.

Pass

Manipulates the state (filter, add, rename fields) without invoking anything.

Choice

Conditional branching.

Wait

Pauses for a duration or until a timestamp.

Parallel

Runs multiple branches at once; collects all results into an array.

Map

Like , but for a dynamic-size array — the same workflow runs once per item.

The 2023+ Distributed Map scales Map up to 10,000 parallel iterations (good for fanning out over big S3 lists, large arrays).

Succeed / Fail

Terminal states.  records an error + cause that the parent can .

Chapter 5 — Retry and Catch

Every  can declare retries and error catchers:

Retries multiply: first the most specific error block runs, then  as a catch-all.

> Trick: put exponential backoff () on Retries that hit external APIs — the third-party will recover; instant retries waste your throttling budget.

Chapter 6 — The Saga pattern

In a distributed system, you can't have ACID across services. The Saga pattern fakes it: every step has a compensating action, and if a later step fails, the workflow runs the prior compensating actions in reverse.

Example: order placement.

If  fails, the workflow:

1. Cancels delivery (n/a, never scheduled).2. Refunds the card.3. Releases the inventory.4. Returns the user a coherent failure response.

In Step Functions, model it as a  block with  that routes to a compensating sub-workflow.

Chapter 7 — Express vs Standard pricing

| Standard | Express ||----------|---------|| USD 0.025 per 1,000 state transitions | USD 1 per 1M executions + GB-second compute time |

A long-running workflow with 50 transitions costs USD 0.00125 per execution on Standard. A 200-ms Express execution at 100 MB costs USD 0.0000004 per execution. Express wins for high-volume; Standard wins for long-running / fewer / audit-required.

Many production systems use both: Standard for the business workflow, Express for inner high-frequency loops.

Chapter 8 — When NOT to use Step Functions

| Scenario | Better fit ||----------|------------|| Single Lambda, no branching | just use Lambda || Pure pub/sub fanout | SNS or EventBridge || Pure queue + worker | SQS + Lambda || Stream processing | Kinesis + Lambda || Simple event chain across AWS services without transformations | EventBridge Pipes (newer, lighter than Step Functions for source → enrich → target) || Always-on long-running daemon | ECS / EC2 |

EventBridge Pipes deserves a special mention: launched 2022, ideal for "read from queue/stream → optional Lambda enrich → write to target". For pure pipelines without branching, Pipes is cheaper and simpler than Step Functions.

Chapter 9 — A worked example: image-processing pipeline

Workflow: user uploads photo → resize multiple variants in parallel → run ML classifier → store metadata → notify user.

What this gains over hand-wired Lambdas:- Visual diagram for every execution.- Built-in retries with backoff.- Direct SDK integration for DynamoDB + SNS — no Lambda wrappers.- Full execution history queryable in the console.- Easy modification — add a step in JSON, no code change.

Chapter 10 — Standard workflow + human approval

A common pattern: wait for a human approval mid-workflow. Step Functions supports callback tokens — pause until an out-of-band call resumes.

The workflow pauses (up to a year on Standard). A human (or webhook) calls  /  with the token to resume.

Practice Exercises

Exercise 1 — Hello state machine

Create a Standard workflow with three Lambdas in sequence: A → B → C. Start an execution; watch the visual diagram in the console.

Exercise 2 — Choice branch

Add a Choice state after A:- if  → Lambda X- else → Lambda Y

Test both inputs.

Exercise 3 — Retry + Catch

Make Lambda B fail intermittently (raise an exception 50% of the time). Add Retry (3 attempts, backoff 2) and Catch (→ a "Notify failure" Lambda). Observe behaviour.

Exercise 4 — Saga

Implement the order-placement Saga from Chapter 6 with three real Lambdas (each can be a stub that prints + returns). Force the third step to fail; verify compensating actions run in reverse.

Exercise 5 — Direct service integration

Replace one of your Task Lambdas with a direct DynamoDB . Confirm the workflow shrinks by one Lambda and runs faster.

Exercise 6 — Express vs Standard

Convert a workflow that runs 1000+ times/day to Express. Compare costs over a week.

Cheat-sheet

| What | How ||------|-----|| Workflow types | Standard (long, audit) vs Express (high-volume, short) || Definition | JSON (Amazon States Language) || Run |  || Task state | invokes Lambda or AWS service directly || Choice | conditional branch || Parallel | run branches concurrently || Map | per-item iteration (Distributed Map for big arrays) || Wait | pause for time or until timestamp || Retry | per-Task; supports exponential backoff || Catch | route on error to recovery branch || Direct integrations | 200+ AWS service actions without Lambda || Callback token | pause for human approval / external system || Saga | every step has a compensating action || EventBridge Pipes | simpler source→enrich→target pipelines || Visual diagram | every execution; jump from console to details |

What's next

Post 07 introduces Observability & Security Baseline — CloudWatch, CloudTrail, Systems Manager, KMS, Secrets Manager, WAF. Now that the workload runs across compute, data, events, and workflows, you need to see what's happening and protect it.

References

- Step Functions Developer Guide- Amazon States Language spec- The AWS Workflow Studio — visual designer.- EventBridge Pipes overview- Serverless Land — Step Functions patterns
